Adiabatic Fredholm Theory
Nathaniel Bottman, Katrin Wehrheim

Abstract
. We develop a robust functional analytic framework for adiabatic limits. This framework consist of a notion of adiabatic Fredholm family, several possible regularity properties, and an explicit construction that provides finite dimensional reductions that fit into all common regularization theories. We show that thhese finite dimensional reductions inherit global continuity and differentiability properties from the adiabatic Fredholm family. Moreover, we indicate how to construct adiabatic Fredholm families that describe the adiabatic limits for the nondegenerate Atiyah-Floer conjecture and strip-shrinking in quilted Floer theory.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
47H09,  53D40,  57R58,  58B15

1.
Introduction

This paper develops an analytic framework for adiabatic limits in geometric analysis – towards systematizing the use of PDE degenerations to relate invariants arising from moduli spaces of solutions of nonlinear elliptic PDEs of quite different types.
Consider the following examples:

Example 1.1
.

[AF]

The (nondegenerate) Atiyah-Floer conjecture
[
DS94
,
Sal95
]
relates an instanton Floer homology (closely related to Donaldson invariants) to a Lagrangian Floer homology by degenerating the anti-self-duality equation on a bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\Sigma
to a Cauchy-Riemann equation for maps
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
to a representation space of a Riemann surface
\Sigma
.

[GW]

The symplectic vortex invariants for a Hamiltonian group action
G\times M\to M
on a symplectic manifold
M
are identified

[
GS05
,
CGMiRS02
]
with Gromov-Witten invariants of the symplectic quotient
M/\!\!/G
– under a number of assumptions, in particular monotonicity –
by degenerating the symplectic vortex equations for maps
\Sigma\to M
coupled with a
G
-connection on a bundle over
\Sigma
to a Cauchy-Riemann equation for maps
\Sigma\to M/\!\!/G
.

[SW]

Relationships between Seiberg-Witten invariants and Heegaard-Floer theory
[
KLT20
]
can be interpreted
[
CGMiRS02
, §10]
as a degeneration of the Seiberg-Witten equations on a bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\Sigma
to a Cauchy-Riemann equation for maps
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to{\rm Sym}^{g}(\Sigma)
to the symmetric product of a Riemann surface
\Sigma
.

[QF]

Quilted Floer theory
[
WW12
]
was shown – under transversality and monotonicity assumptions – to be invariant under geometric composition of Lagrangian correspondences, by a degeneration of the Cauchy-Riemann equation for tuples of strips of varying width (whose boundaries are coupled via Lagrangian conditions) – allowing widths to go to zero, and replacing the shrunk strip by composition of the associated Lagrangian correspondences.

Examples [AF] and [QF] will be described in more detail in §
3
.
Each of these degenerations can be described locally by a smooth family of nonlinear Fredholm maps (or elliptic differential operators)
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
with fixed domain and target for
\epsilon>0
that are related to an energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}:\overline{\Gamma}\to[0,\infty)
with the property that solutions have a fixed energy
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=0\;\Rightarrow\;\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=E_{0}
that is
\epsilon
-independent. In the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, however, the energy functional diverges on most of
\overline{\Gamma}
– with the exception of a subspace
\Gamma_{0}:=\{\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}\,|\,\sup_{\epsilon>0}E_{\epsilon}(\gamma)<\infty\}\subset\overline{\Gamma}
. Corresponding, the differential operators
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
diverge on most of
\overline{\Gamma}
, but their restrictions to
\Gamma_{0}
have a well-defined limit
\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}|_{\Gamma_{0}}
.
The limit map (or operator)
\mathcal{F}_{0}:\Gamma_{0}\to\overline{\Omega}
has the same energy of solutions
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\gamma)=0\;\Rightarrow\;\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=E_{0}
, although it is no longer Fredholm (or elliptic). However, there are projections
\Gamma_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma^{\rm red}_{0}
and

\overline{\Omega}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega^{\rm red}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{0}
descends to a an operator
\mathcal{F}^{\rm red}_{0}:\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}\to\overline{\Omega}^{\rm red}_{0}
that – after completion – is Fredholm (or elliptic) with the same index as
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
.

Remark 1.2

(The notion “adiabatic”)
.

The name “adiabatic limit” or “adiabatic degeneration” that has been used for these examples was probably motivated by some or all of the notions

\cdot

adiabatic change in mechanics – a slow deformation of the Hamiltonian,

\cdot

adiabatic invariants in dynamical systems – stay approximately constant under slow change,

\cdot

adiabatic processes in thermodynamics – that don’t increase the entropy,

\cdot

adiabatic change in quantum mechanics – where energy states cannot transition.

In the context of geometric analysis, we suggest using these terms when both an energy and a Fredholm index are preserved – akin to an adiabatic invariant – which prevents major changes of geometric characteristics in the limit. We would add the common characteristic of this preservation taking place despite of a serious analytic degeneration – e.g. quantities diverging, Fredholmness being lost, and being regained only after restriction to a smaller “state space”.

We will give a technical definition of “adiabatic Fredholm family” in Definition
2.1
, which is highly specific to our purposes and results from a significant reformulation of the classical examples. It does not include an energy function, as this is more relevant to compactness arguments than the construction of local charts. Instead, the key property of an “adiabatic Fredholm limit" is that Fredholmness holds relative to a family of
\epsilon
-dependent norms, which are bounded below but not equivalent to the
\epsilon=0
norm.
Note that we do not intend to restrict the use of the term “adiabatic” to this specific meaning – rather use it as an adjective to indicate the generalization of a classical notion that serves to better analyze some adiabatic limits.

The (Floer-type / Gromov-Witten / …) invariants arising from the PDEs in Example
1.1
are constructed by giving the moduli spaces of PDE solutions (modulo symmetries and after compactification) the structure of a space that can be counted (e.g. a compact oriented
0
-manifold), integrated over, or associated with some type of fundamental class. For a moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\epsilon}
arising from a PDE with fixed
\epsilon\geq 0
, various types of such “regularization structures" have been constructed – all arising from local Fredholm descriptions
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
as described above.
Now any “regularization theory" (a method for assigning counts or (virtual/relative/…) fundamental classes to moduli spaces) crucially relies on the fact that Fredholm descriptions have transverse stabilizations. These are used differently by the various theories, but a key fact in all theories is that the stabilizations exist and induce “finite dimensional reductions" in the following sense.

Remark 1.3
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
be a topological space.
Then a
local Fredholm description
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
consists of a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Fredholm
1
1

1

A nonlinear map is called Fredholm if its linearizations
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{0})
are Fredholm at each zero
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0)
.

map
\mathcal{F}:\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
between Banach spaces and a continuous injection
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}
that is a homeomorphism onto its image. (More precisely,
\mathcal{F}
is defined on an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\Gamma
.)

Since the cokernel of a Fredholm map is finite dimensional, any such local Fredholm description can be extended to a
stabilized Fredholm map

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}:\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
that is transverse to
0
(after restriction to a smaller domain). Here
\mathfrak{C}
is a finite dimensional vector space that is either isomorphic to
\operatorname{coker}{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{0})
or can be viewed to contain it as a subspace, and transversality guarantees that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(0)
is a finite dimensional manifold equipped with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
map
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(0)\to\mathfrak{C}
whose zero set is identified with
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0)
.
This fact can be viewed as “local obstruction bundle”, source of “transverse perturbations" (arising from the regular values of
\pi
), or a “finite dimensional reduction”.

More generally, a
finite dimensional reduction
of a local Fredholm description is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
map
f:\mathfrak{K}\to\mathfrak{C}
between finite dimensional vector spaces and a continuous injection
\phi:f^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{-1}(0)
that is a homeomorphism onto its image.
A review of the classical construction of finite dimensional reductions – in a less well-known formulation that we developed for the adiabatic generalization – can be found in Lemmas
4.4
,
4.6
,
4.9
,
4.11
.

Thus any local Fredholm description, via stabilization and finite dimensional reduction, induces a
local finite dimensional description
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
that consists of a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
map
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a continuous injection
f^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}
that is a homeomorphism onto its image.

Once finite dimensional descriptions of a moduli space are constructed, they induce “local regularization structures" (e.g. local Euler classes or local perturbations), and the content of a regularization theory is to patch these into a global regularization structure for the moduli space. Then invariants are defined by counting or integrating “regularized moduli spaces" (and packaging the results in an algebraic structure than can be shown to be independent of choices.)

Now the idea for identifying invariants for
\epsilon=1
and
\epsilon=0
is that the union of moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{[0,1]}:=\bigcup_{0\leq\epsilon\leq 1}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\epsilon}
could be equipped with a regularization structure that plays the role of a cobordism relating the invariants arising from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
.
The crucial analytic step in such a program is to stabilize the local Fredholm descriptions

\mathcal{F}_{0}^{{\rm red}\;-1}(0)\cup\bigcup_{0<\epsilon\leq 1}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{[0,1]}

and perform finite dimensional reduction to obtain a local finite dimensional description of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{[0,1]}
.
To date, such description for an adiabatic limit has been achieved only in the special case of
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{{\rm red}\;-1}(0)
starting out as a
0
-manifold arising from
\mathcal{F}_{0}
being transverse to
0
.
2
2

2

Transversality in this case of Fredholm index
0
means that at each solution
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\gamma)=0
the linearization
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(\gamma)
is an isomorphism.

In that case no stabilization is necessary and a bijection
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\simeq\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\epsilon}
for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
is established in two steps by the

classical adiabatic method
(for transverse problems of Fredholm index
0
):

\bullet

Given any solution
\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{{\rm red}\;-1}(0)
one constructs a family of solutions
\gamma_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)
for
\epsilon>0
by utilizing a lift of
\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}
to
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
to obtain “near-solutions”:
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Then for sufficiently small
\epsilon
a Newton-Picard iteration finds exact solutions
\gamma_{0}+\xi_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)
.
(See e.g.
[
DS94
, Thm.5.1]
,
[
WW12
, Thm.3.1.1]
.)

\bullet

A compactness theorem (e.g.
[
DS94
, Thm.9.2]
,
[
WW12
, Thm.3.3.1]
,
[
BW18
]
) shows that solutions
\gamma_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)
for
\epsilon\to 0
will converge to a lift
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
of some solution
\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{{\rm red}\;-1}(0)
.

This can also be viewed as constructing a
1
-cobordism
\bigcup_{\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{{\rm red}\;-1}(0)}\{\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}\}\cup\bigcup_{0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}}\{\gamma_{0}+\xi_{\epsilon}\}
with boundary
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{{\rm red}\;-1}(0)\sqcup\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}^{-1}(0)
. However, the local finite dimensional descriptions of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{[0,1]}
constructed here are the trivial functions
f:[0,\epsilon_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}^{0}=\{0\}
with a nontrivial injection
[0,\epsilon_{0}]\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{[0,1]}
, which maps
0\mapsto\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}
and
0<\epsilon\mapsto\gamma_{0}+\xi_{\epsilon}
. This injection is constructed by working only with solutions of the PDEs – thus provides no hints on how to lift a stabilization or finite dimensional reduction of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm red}_{0}:\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}\to\overline{\Omega}^{\rm red}_{0}
to a construction for
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
.

Remark 1.4

(The broken dream of using sc-retracts)
.

Ever since polyfold theory
[
HWZ21
]
entered the market of ideas in 2004, we have expected to eventually obtain finite dimensional descriptions for adiabatic limits
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}\to\mathcal{F}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\rm red}_{0}
by casting them as a single sc-Fredholm section over a polyfold of the form
\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}\cup\bigcup_{0<\epsilon\leq 1}\overline{\Gamma}
. After all, the very first examples of “sc-retracts” can be used to describe this kind of base space as the image of a family of projections
(\pi_{\epsilon}:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E})_{\epsilon\in[0,1]}
with
\operatorname{im}\pi_{0}\simeq\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}
and
\operatorname{im}\pi_{\epsilon}\simeq\overline{\Gamma}
for
\epsilon>0
.
However, the ambient “sc-space” for this family is, essentially,
\mathbb{E}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\overline{\Gamma}
, and – despite significant efforts – we have been unable to find an extension of the adiabatic family of PDEs to this or any other ambient space of an sc-retract describing the adiabatic limit.

We have found that a crucial difference between gluing constructions – for which polyfold theory offers a robust analytic framework – and the Newton-Picard iteration used for adiabatic limits is the following: Gluing constructions use the Newton-Picard iteration to find a PDE solution near a pre-glued map
\oplus_{R}(u_{a},u_{b})
, which interpolates between two PDE solutions
u_{a},u_{b}
on a neck of length
R
.
Now this pregluing map
(R,u_{a},u_{b})\mapsto\oplus_{R}(u_{a},u_{b})
is well-defined for any pair of maps
u_{a},u_{b}
, and is accompanied by a similar map
(R,u_{a},u_{b})\mapsto\ominus_{R}(u_{a},u_{b})
which keeps track of the information lost by
\oplus_{R}
.
3
3

3

In fact
(\oplus_{R},\ominus_{R})
is an isomorphism; see
[
FFGW16
, §2.3]
for a survey of this core idea of polyfold theory.

Together, these serve to reinterpret the pregluing construction as a chart for the ambient space
\Gamma_{\infty}\cup\bigcup_{R\geq 1}\Gamma_{R}
of maps from domains with neck length
1\leq R\leq\infty
. The total space for this sc-retract is
[1,\infty]\times\Gamma_{\infty}
– thus supports the same type of elliptic PDE as the gluing problem.

For adiabatic limits the analogue of the pregluing is the lift
(\epsilon,\gamma^{\rm red}_{0})\mapsto\gamma_{0}
. However, this is a well-defined map only on a dense subspace of
\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}
which contains all smooth maps, in particular the PDE solutions. Even if these lifts did cover
\Gamma_{0}
, we would then need a direct sum
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}\oplus\Gamma_{\ominus}
to mimic the construction of the sc-retract from
(\oplus,\ominus)
. Such splittings, however, have been elusive for the adiabatic limits
[AF]
,
[QF]
that we studied in detail.

The gauge-theoretic Examples
1.1
[AF], [SW] are naturally monotone and can be regularized with geometric methods, so a satisfying level of generality is achievable with the classical adiabatic method. However, the inherently symplectic Examples [GW], [QF] are severely limited by the monotonicity assumption, which is required both to avoid bubbling (which in both cases is understood to algebraically obstruct the desired result) and to ensure that transversality of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm red}_{0}
can be achieved by a geometric method – i.e. one that is compatible with the classical method.
For Example [QF] the algebraic impact of bubbling has now been understood and cast into the first author’s proposal of the symplectic
(A_{\infty},2)
-category
[
AB24
]
. And with the compactness theorem long established
[
BW18
]
, a local finite dimensional description for the moduli spaces near
\epsilon=0
for Example
1.1
[QF] became the only foundational piece missing for a systematic description of the functorial properties of Fukaya categories. The present paper fills this gap.

Such a general description of the moduli spaces of quilted Floer trajectories (solutions of several Cauchy-Riemann equations, coupled by Lagrangian seam conditions; see §
3
for details) in the case of one strip-width going to zero requires extending the adiabatic “strip-shrinking” analysis in
[
WW12
]
to cases where the moduli spaces are not a priori cut out transversely, and of any expected dimension – i.e. Fredholm index.
Moreover, the proposed symplectic
(A_{\infty},2)
-category also requires extending this adiabatic analysis to multiple strips shrinking to width zero – at any ratio of speeds.
And for the rapidly growing number of proposed applications of pseudoholomorphic quilts – which utilize more general domains, symplectic fibrations, shrinking of annuli, and possibly even more sophisticated degenerations – each use case requires its own version of this adiabatic analysis.
Finally, any non-monotone use case requires coupling the requisite adiabatic analysis with the gluing analysis for the four types of bubbles exhibited in
[
BW18
]
– in particular the figure-eight bubbles, of which any number can appear simultaneously in a top boundary stratum. To serve all those use cases in a non-partisan manner – i.e. without restricting the user to a particular regularization theory – the goal of this paper is to develop a robust functional analytic framework for adiabatic limits that

(a)

applies at least to Example
1.1
[QF], ideally to more of the known examples;

(b)

provides finite dimensional reductions that fit into all common regularization theories;

(c)

is compatible with all common approaches to gluing.

We propose such a framework with the notion of an
adiabatic Fredholm family
in Definition
2.1
.
Note that it would be relatively easy to create a definition that satisfies just one of the goals (a) or (b). To avoid this common issue of new theoretical frameworks, we used goal (a) as a guardrail in developing this notion, while striving to meet goal (b) by proving Theorem
1.8
below – i.e. in working towards finite dimensional reductions, we allowed ourselves to add conditions to Definition
2.1
only when these were satisfied in Example
1.1
[QF]. As such, the actual definition has become quite technical, so here is a simplified version that combines Definitions
2.1
,
2.13
for
\mathcal{C}^{\ell=1}
-regularity.

Simplified Definition:

A
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-regular adiabatic Fredholm family
consists of

•

\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\Gamma\to\Omega\bigr)_{\epsilon\in\Delta}
a family of maps between real vector spaces indexed by a topological space
\Delta
with a distinguished point
0=0_{\Delta}\in\Delta
such that
\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)=0
;

•

families of norms
\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
on
\Gamma
and
\Omega
, respectively, indexed by
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

These are required to have the following properties.

[Lower Bound on Norms]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\leq\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and

\|\omega\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq\|\omega\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\omega\in\Omega
, and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Uniform
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Regularity]

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
and satisfies

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\leq C^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\leq c^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}}-\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\;\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}},\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\in\Gamma

with a constant
C^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}\geq 1
and a monotone continuous function
c^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
c^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}(0)=0
.

[Fredholm Property & Constant Index]

The linearizations at
0=0_{\Gamma}
extend to Fredholm operators
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
between the completions
\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{\Gamma}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{\Omega}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

Their Fredholm index is independent of
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[
\mathbf{\epsilon=0}
Fredholm Estimate & Uniform Cokernel Bound]

There is a projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{K}:=\ker\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
and an inclusion
\mathfrak{C}:=\operatorname{coker}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\subset\Omega
with

\sup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\sup_{\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq 1}\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}<\infty
such that

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}+\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq C_{0}\bigl(\|\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)\|^{\mathfrak{K}}+\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\gamma-\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\bigr)\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\;(\gamma,\mathfrak{c})\in\Gamma\times\mathfrak{C}.

[Uniform Fredholm-ish Estimate]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{1}\bigl(\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\bigr)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Pointwise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-continuity w.r.t.
\mathbf{\Delta}
]

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\Omega}_{0})}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}

and, given any
\epsilon_{0}\in\Delta
and solutions
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
of
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})=\mathfrak{c}_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}=\mathfrak{c}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have

\bigl\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})-\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\underset{\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}}{\longrightarrow}0.

[Regularizing]

The nonlinear and linearized operators are regularizing in the sense that

\displaystyle\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\;\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\in\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\in\Gamma,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma,\;\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\;{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma\in\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\in\Gamma.

For users wishing to avoid noncomplete normed vector spaces, the alternative formulation in terms of an extended adiabatic Fredholm family can be found in Lemma
2.2
. However, the regularizing property will be crucial for constructing finite dimensional reductions that are continuous in
\epsilon\in\Delta
.
Defined formally in Definition
2.8
, the regularizing property encodes elliptic regularity of the PDEs – similar to
[
HWZ21
, Def.3.1.16]
.
4
4

4

The regularizing property is unfortunately named similarly, but should not be confused with regularization – the process of associating well-defined counts or fundamental classes to moduli spaces, as e.g. surveyed in
[
FFGW16
, §3]
.

Remark 1.5

(Applicability to examples of adiabatic limits)
.

In the application to Examples
1.1
,

\bullet

\Gamma,\Omega
are spaces of smooth connections or maps – equipped with
\epsilon
-dependent Sobolev norms,

\bullet

\Delta=[0,\epsilon_{0}]
, though our formulation allows for
\Delta
to be any chart of an underlying moduli space of domains – including one with generalized corners,

\bullet

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
represent the
\epsilon
-dependent PDEs in a local slice.
5
5

5

For gauge theoretic symmetries one usually builds gauge fixing into the PDE. For quotients by reparametrization one usually builds local slice conditions into the domain.

The original formulations, including the above abstract summary, are not of this form since the PDEs contain negative exponents of
\epsilon
which diverge for
\epsilon\to 0
. They can, however, be brought into this form by multiplying with a suitable positive exponent of
\epsilon
such that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}=:\mathcal{F}_{0}
exists – and combines the
\epsilon=0
PDE with the condition for solutions to be contained in
\Gamma_{0}
.
Here our breakthrough discovery was the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is Fredholm on a completion of the same space as
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
– just after completion with a non-equivalent norm – in fact, a Sobolev norm that is significantly weaker than the one used in the classical adiabatic method for

\mathcal{F}^{\rm red}_{0}:\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}\to\overline{\Omega}^{\rm red}_{0}
, which works on a “smaller” Banach space with a stronger norm than the new Fredholm operator
\mathcal{F}_{0}:\overline{\Gamma}_{0}\to\overline{\Omega}_{0}
.

Remark 1.6

(Compatibility with common approaches to gluing)
.

Regarding goal (c), the descriptions of the Examples
1.1
in terms of adiabatic Fredholm families utilize the classical Fredholm descriptions for fixed
\epsilon>0
, which are compatible with both classical and polyfold gluing methods as the pregluing construction
\oplus_{R}(u_{a},u_{b})
(sketched in Remark
1.4
) can be applied to smooth maps
u_{a},u_{b}
and yields smooth maps. It can similarly be applied to transfer representations of the cokernel from
R=\infty
to finite gluing parameters
R
.
The main challenge is in ensuring that the new Fredholm description for
\epsilon=0
is also compatible with the gluing analysis – in fact, is the limit of
\epsilon\searrow 0
.

As a result, we will be dealing with Fredholm problems parametrized by
\epsilon\in\Delta
and gluing parameters
R
– with the notable exception of gluing figure eight bubbles into shrinking strips, where the strip width
\epsilon>0
determines the gluing parameter
R
that will match seams. This at least heuristically aligns with the construction of
(A_{\infty},2)
-associahedra in
[
Bot19
]
.

Remark 1.7

(Relationship to the classical adiabatic method)
.

The analytic ingredients of the Newton-Picard iteration in the classical adiabatic method are represented in the above definition as follows:
The “near solutions" are given by
[partial
\mathcal{C}^{0}
-continuity w.r.t.
\mathbf{\Delta}
]
applied to
\gamma_{0}=0
. The existence of bounded right inverses is classically proven by estimates for the formal adjoint operators
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)^{*}
that are obtained by combining “quadratic estimates" – equivalent to
[uniform
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity]
with a linear function
c^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}
– with
[uniform Fredholm-ish estimates]
and the fact that the linearized operator for
\epsilon=0
is an isomorphism
\overline{\Gamma}^{\rm red}_{0}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Omega^{\rm red}_{0}
, which is represented by estimates for the formal adjoint operator
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}^{*}
– a part of the classical adiabatic analysis that wasn’t well positioned for generalizations.

Here our breakthrough insight was that the isomorphism property of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
in the classical case
could be understood as a special case of an isomorphism property of a Fredholm stabilization coupled with projection to the kernel,

(1)

\overline{P}_{0}\,:\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\Gamma}_{0}\times\mathfrak{C}\;\to\;\mathfrak{K}\times\overline{\Omega}_{0},\hskip 20.00003pt(\gamma,\mathfrak{c})\;\mapsto\;\bigl(\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma),\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\gamma-\mathfrak{c}\bigr).

For a general adiabatic Fredholm family, this isomorphism is represented by the
[
\epsilon=0
Fredholm Estimate]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}+\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq C_{0}\bigl(\|\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)\|^{\mathfrak{K}}+\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\gamma-\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\bigr)
, which implies injectivity of
\overline{P}_{0}
, so that surjectivity follows from
\overline{P}_{0}
having Fredholm index
0
.

This turned out to be an analytically more robust formulation than the classical notion of a Fredholm splitting, in which we have direct sum decompositions of the domain
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}=\mathfrak{K}\oplus W
and the target
\overline{\Omega}_{0}=\mathfrak{C}\oplus\operatorname{im}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
so that the restricted operator
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}|_{W}
is an isomorphism to its image.

A general adiabatic Fredholm family just needs to be defined on a convex subset
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\Gamma
containing
0=0_{\Gamma}
such that
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{0})
is open.
Now the main result of this paper is that the new notion of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-regular adiabatic Fredholm family (a) is satisfied by Examples
1.1
as shown in §
3
, and (b) induces local finite dimensional reductions as follows:

Theorem 1.8
.

Suppose an open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}
of a topological space (such as a compactified moduli space) is described as the zero set of a
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
as in Definitions
2.1
,
2.13
for
\ell\geq 0
.
That is, there is a homeomorphism

\psi\,:\;\textstyle\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\;\overset{\sim}{\to}\;\mathcal{U}.

Then this induces a finite dimensional reduction that describes
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
locally as the zero set of a map between finite dimensional spaces,

f:\Delta_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathfrak{C},\;(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\mapsto f_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\psi_{f}:f^{-1}(0)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}.

More precisely,

\mathfrak{K}=\ker{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\subset\Gamma
is the kernel and
\mathfrak{C}\simeq\operatorname{coker}{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\subset\Omega
is the cokernel of the linearization of
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon=0}
at
0\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\Gamma
.
Then the finite dimensional reduction is defined on open subsets
\Delta_{\sigma}\subset\Delta
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathfrak{K}
. It describes
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
locally by composition
\psi_{f}=\psi\circ\phi
with a homeomorphism for some
\delta_{\sigma}>0

\phi\,:\;f^{-1}(0)\;\overset{\sim}{\to}\;\bigl(\textstyle\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{\sigma}}\{\epsilon\}\times\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\bigr)\cap\bigl\{(\epsilon,\gamma)\,\big|\,\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}<\delta_{\sigma}\bigr\}\;\subset\;\Delta\times\bigl(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|_{0}\bigr).

Moreover, this finite dimensional reduction is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
in the sense that the differentials of order
0\leq k\leq\ell
(from
k
-fold differentiation in
\mathfrak{K}
; see Remark
2.3
) form continuous maps

\Delta_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathcal{L}^{k}(\mathfrak{K}^{k},\mathfrak{C}),\hskip 10.00002pt(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k}_{0})\mapsto{\rm D}^{k}f_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}_{0}).

Remark 1.9

(Prior Results)
.

The prior adiabatic limit proofs in Examples
1.1
work with
\mathfrak{K}=\{0\}
,
\mathfrak{C}=\{0\}
, and hence the trivial maps
f:\Delta_{\sigma}=[0,\epsilon_{\sigma})\to\{0\}
. Their Newton-Picard iteration induces maps
\phi:f^{-1}(0)=[0,\epsilon_{\sigma})\to\bigl\{(\epsilon,\gamma)\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=0,\|\gamma-\gamma_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}<\delta_{\sigma}\bigr\}
for each
\gamma^{\rm red}_{0}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm red\;-1}(0)
, for which continuity and injectivity for fixed
\epsilon
holds vacuously since the domain is a single point. Continuity for varying
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{\sigma})
was not even a reasonable question for lack of an ambient topological space.

Remark 1.10

(Fit with common regularization theories)
.

Regarding goal (b) – finite dimensional reductions that fit into all common regularization theories –
note that the finite dimensional descriptions
f:\Delta_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathfrak{C}
of moduli spaces resulting from an adiabatic Fredholm family via Theorem
1.8
come in the form of Remark
1.3
up to restricting to an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathfrak{K}
(a standard modification) and introducing an extra factor
\Delta_{\sigma}
– in applications a chart of an underlying finite dimensional moduli space of domains.
While we obtain no differentiability in the directions of
\Delta_{\sigma}
, the derivatives in directions of
\mathfrak{K}
are continuous with respect to
\Delta_{\sigma}
.
Indeed, for a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-regular adiabatic Fredholm family, the assertion of Theorem
1.8
is the continuity of both the map

\Delta_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathfrak{C}
,
(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\mapsto f_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k})

and the differential in the direction of
\mathfrak{K}
, in the sense that

\Delta_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{C})
,
(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k}_{0})\mapsto{\rm D}f_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}_{0})
is a continuous map to the space of bounded linear operators
\mathfrak{K}\to\mathfrak{C}
.

Since the work of Siebert
[
Sie96
, §1]
this differentiability in all but finitely many directions has been widely understood as the optimal requirement for local regularization structures.
In fact, the relinquishing of differentiability in finitely many directions is also a core technique in proofs of the polyfold theoretic sc-Fredholm property; see
[
HWZ17
, Prop.4.23]
for the Gromov-Witten case and
[
Weh12
, Def.4.3]
for a general formulation.
A regularization theory just needs local regularization structures with sufficiently large spaces of perturbations or stabilizing factors to cover the cokernels of the differentials in the differentiable directions. (Thus we do need some differentiable directions.)
Moreover, a regularization theory needs to compatibly patch these local regularization structures – which, however, is formulated quite differently in different theories, so remains the job of the user.

A more general version of Theorem
1.8
is stated in Corollary
4.12
.
The proof takes up §
4
and proceeds as follows: §
4.1
lifts the stabilization isomorphism (
1
) to
\epsilon\neq 0
. §
4.2
utilizes these isomorphisms to rewrite the equations

(2)

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=0\hskip 20.00003pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\bigl(A_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w),w-B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)\bigr)=(0,0),

where each
B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\cdot)
is a contraction mapping – analogous to the notion of an sc-basic germ in
[
HWZ21
, Def.3.1.7]
. §
4.3
constructs solution maps
\sigma_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to W=\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
that solve the fixed point equations
w=B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)
and shows how various regularity assumptions on
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
transfer to the contractions
B_{\epsilon}
and then the solution maps
\sigma_{\epsilon}
. This is where the technical heart of our work is in the interplay of Definition
2.13
with Theorem
4.10
and Example
1.1
: While it is relatively easy to formulate a definition of “adiabatic regularity" that implies regularity of the solution maps, or to capture the regularity properties of the examples in a notion of “adiabatic regularity", the crucial contribution of this paper is in finding a notion of adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity that (a) is satisfied in Examples
1.1
and (b) implies via Theorem
4.10
the adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity of the solution maps.
Here, in addition, the notion of adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{0}
regularity was designed to ensure global continuity of the solution map
(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\to\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
with respect to the weakest norm
\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
.
These results are analogous to the properties of the solution germs in
[
HWZ21
, Thm.3.3.3]
, thus make the case that adiabatic Fredholm families fit into the polyfold regularization theory.

To fit adiabatic Fredholm families into regularization theories via finite dimensional charts or obstruction bundles we deduce in §
4.4
that (
2
) is equivalent to a finite dimensional equation

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=0\hskip 20.00003pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptA_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}))=0.

Here the finite dimensional reduction map
f:\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{k}\mapsto A_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}))
is defined on an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathfrak{K}
of the kernel
\mathfrak{K}=\ker\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
, maps to the cokernel
\mathfrak{C}=\operatorname{coker}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
, and inherits the regularity of the adiabatic Fredholm family.

To make this analytic work both accessible and educational, we formulate a classical analogue of
Theorem
1.8
in Theorem
4.1
– the fact that every
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Fredholm map has a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
finite dimensional reduction – and begin each section with a review of our version of the construction step in classical Fredholm theory before going into the new technical work of making the construction uniformly and compatibly for all
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

We are most grateful to MPIM Bonn for physically hosting – and thus making possible – the breakthrough phase of our work.
The second author also wishes to acknowledge the annoying accuracy of Dietmar Salamon’s oracle that “it’s just estimates".

2.
Adiabatic Fredholm Families

This section develops the notions of an adiabatic Fredholm family and its possible regularity properites.
We begin with the least restrictive notion – which will suffice for the existence of invertible Fredholm stabilizations §
4.1
, yielding contractions §
4.2
, solution maps §
4.3
, and finite dimensional reductions §
4.4
, but guaranteeing only continuity and differentiability for fixed
\epsilon\in\Delta
– no regularity for varying
\epsilon
. The latter will require further properties of the adiabatic Fredholm family that are developed in §
2.2
.

Definition 2.1
.

An
adiabatic Fredholm family
consists of the following data.

•

\Gamma
and
\Omega
are real vector spaces.
6
6

6

These are usually infinite dimensional and we do not specify a norm or topology at this point. In our applications, these are spaces of smooth functions, which we will later complete w.r.t.
\epsilon
-dependent Sobolev norms.

•

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\Gamma
is a convex subset containing
0=0_{\Gamma}\in\Gamma
.
7
7

7

In our applications, this is a small open
ball in a
\mathcal{C}^{0}
-norm that is weaker than any of the
\epsilon
-dependent norms.

•

\Delta
is a topological space with a distinguished point
0=0_{\Delta}\in\Delta
.
8
8

8

This is usually a local chart for a finite dimensional topological manifold. In our applications, it will be
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times[0,1)^{n}
or a generalized corner of an underlying moduli space of domains.

•

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega
is a family of maps indexed by
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

•

\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
are families of norms on
\Gamma
and
\Omega
, respectively, indexed by
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

•

\mathfrak{K}\subset\Gamma
and
\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
are finite dimensional subspaces
equipped with the norms
\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{\mathfrak{K}}:=\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
for
\mathfrak{k}\in\mathfrak{K}
and
\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}:=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}
for
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
.

•

\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{K}
is a linear projection.

•

C_{0},C_{1},C_{\mathfrak{C}}\in(0,\infty)
are constants.

•

c:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a monotone continuous function with
c(0)=0
, and

c_{\Delta}:\Delta\to[0,\infty)
is a continuous function with
c_{\Delta}(0)=0
.

These are required to have the following properties.

[Openness of Domain]

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{0})
is open.

[Lower Bound on Norms]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\leq\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and

\|\omega\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq\|\omega\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\omega\in\Omega
, and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Regularity]

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Fredholm]

The linearization
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0):\Gamma\to\Omega
at
0=0_{\Gamma}
for each fixed
\epsilon\in\Delta
extends to a Fredholm operator
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
between the completions
\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{\Gamma}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{\Omega}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}}
.

[Index]

The Fredholm index of the linearizations
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)}
is independent of
\epsilon\in\Delta
.
Moreover, the kernel
\ker\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{0}
is contained in the dense subset
\Gamma
and equals to the given subspace
\mathfrak{K}=\ker\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\subset\Gamma
.
The cokernel
\operatorname{coker}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}=\overline{\Omega}_{0}/\operatorname{Im}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
is isomorphic to the given subspace
\operatorname{coker}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\simeq\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
.
As a result we have

{\operatorname{ind}}\,\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)}=\dim\mathfrak{C}-\dim\mathfrak{K}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[
\mathbf{\epsilon=0}
Fredholm Estimate]

The projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{K}
and inclusion
\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
provide a Fredholm stabilization of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
in the sense that

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}+\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}\leq C_{0}\bigl(\|\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)\|^{\mathfrak{K}}+\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\gamma-\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\bigr)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all}\;(\gamma,\mathfrak{c})\in\Gamma\times\mathfrak{C}.

[Uniform Fredholm-ish Estimate]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{1}\bigl(\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\bigr)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Uniform Cokernel Bound]

\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{\mathfrak{C}}\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}=C_{\mathfrak{C}}\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
for all
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Quadratic-ish Estimate]

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\leq c(\|\gamma_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
for all
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Continuity of Derivatives at
0
]

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\gamma\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq c_{\Delta}(\epsilon)\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Near-Solution]

\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)=0
, that is
\gamma=0
solves the equation
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=0\in\Omega
for
\epsilon=0
.
Moreover, the map
\Delta\to\Omega
,
\epsilon\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)
is continuous at
\epsilon=0
in the sense that
\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}=\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)-\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

An application-minded reader may now want to skip to §
3
to get an idea of what such an adiabatic Fredholm family looks like in practice.

2.1.
Extension to Completions and Tangent Map Notation

Users content with Definition
2.1
can skip most this section, as the extension to Banach space completions is mostly a technical step in the proof.
Users wishing to avoid noncomplete normed vector spaces can work directly with the following notion of an extended adiabatic Fredholm family – a family of Fredholm maps between Banach spaces that have common dense subspaces.
Users wishing to make sense of adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity for
\ell\geq 2
will need the tangent map notation introduced in the later part of this section.

Lemma 2.2
.

An adiabatic Fredholm family as in Definition
2.1
induces the following data of an
extended adiabatic Fredholm family
.

•

\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{\Gamma}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{\Omega}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}}
are Banach spaces obtained by completing the fixed spaces
\Gamma
and
\Omega
w.r.t. norms that vary with
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

•

\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}:=\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}

and

\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}:={\rm int}(\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}})\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
are convex subsets containing
0=0_{\Gamma}\in\Gamma\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
. These are obtained by
taking the closure of a fixed convex subset
0\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\Gamma\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
, and by thaking the interior of this closure.

•

\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
are maps obtained by continuous extension of the maps
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\Gamma\to\Omega
.

•

\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
are norms on
\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
, obtained by continuous extension of the norms specified on
\Gamma
and
\Omega
.

•

\mathfrak{K}\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\mathfrak{C}\subset\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
are inclusions of the fixed finite dimensional subspaces
\mathfrak{K}\subset\Gamma
and
\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
,
equipped with the norms
\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{\mathfrak{K}}=\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
for
\mathfrak{k}\in\mathfrak{K}
and
\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}
for
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
.

•

\overline{\pi}_{\mathfrak{K}}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{K}
is a linear projection obtained by continuous extension of
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\mathfrak{K},\|\cdot\|^{\mathfrak{K}})
.

•

C_{0},C_{1},C_{\mathfrak{C}}\in(0,\infty)
are the same constants as in Definition
2.1
.

•

c:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a monotone continuous function with
c(0)=0
, and

c_{\Delta}:\Delta\to[0,\infty)
is a continuous function with
c_{\Delta}(0)=0
– the same as in Definition
2.1
.

These inherit the following properties.

[Lower Bound on Norms]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\leq\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and

\|\omega\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq\|\omega\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

for all
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
,
\omega\in\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
, and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Openness of Domain]

\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
is open for every
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Regularity]

\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Fredholm]

\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
linearizes at
0=0_{\Gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
to the Fredholm operator
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)=\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Index]

The Fredholm index of these linearizations is
{\operatorname{ind}}\,{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)=\dim\mathfrak{C}-\dim\mathfrak{K}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
, where
\mathfrak{K}=\ker{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)
and
\mathfrak{C}\simeq\operatorname{coker}{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)
.

[
\mathbf{\epsilon=0}
Fredholm Estimate]

The projection
\overline{\pi}_{\mathfrak{K}}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{K}
and inclusion
\mathfrak{C}\subset\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
provide a Fredholm stabilization of
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)
in the sense that

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}+\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}\leq C_{0}\bigl(\|\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)\|^{\mathfrak{K}}+\|{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)\gamma-\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}\bigr)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all}\;(\gamma,\mathfrak{c})\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\times\mathfrak{C}.

[Uniform Fredholm-ish Estimate]

\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{1}\bigl(\|{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\bigr)
for all
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Uniform Cokernel Bound]

\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{\mathfrak{C}}\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}=C_{\mathfrak{C}}\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}
for all
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Quadratic-ish Estimate]

\bigl\|{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma-{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\leq c(\|\gamma_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
for all
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}
,
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
, and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Continuity of Derivatives at
0
]

\bigl\|{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma-{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)\gamma\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq c_{\Delta}(\epsilon)\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
for all
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
and
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

[Near-Solution]

\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)=0
, that is
\gamma=0
solves the equation
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=0\in\Omega
for
\epsilon=0
.
Moreover, the map
\Delta\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
,
\epsilon\mapsto\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)
is continuous at
\epsilon=0
in the sense that
\|\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}=\|\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)-\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

The crucial fact for this Lemma is the fact that the a uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
map between normed vector spaces extends continuously to a
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
map between the completed vector spaces – as shown in Lemma
2.4
.
In particular, the
\ell
-th differential
{\rm D}^{\ell}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
is given by the continuous extension of

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}),\gamma\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)}
, where each
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{\ell}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
is the continuous extension of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{\ell}(\gamma):\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{\ell}\to\Omega_{\epsilon}
.
Here
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}:={\rm int}(\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}})
is the interior of the closure
\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
.

The rest of this proof is a useful exercise in getting familiar with the conditions for adiabatic Fredholm families – and how they each interact with the norms, completions, and continuous extensions.
Note in particular that the linear projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\mathfrak{K},\|\cdot\|^{\mathfrak{K}})
is automatically bounded since
\mathfrak{K}
is finite dimensional.
∎

Here and throughout we are using the notions of Fréchet differential and Fréchet differentiability classes – reviewed in the following remark.

Remark 2.3
.

Throughout, we consider normed vector spaces
(V,\|\cdot\|^{V})
and
(W,\|\cdot\|^{W})
and a continuous map
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
defined on an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{V}\subset V
.

\bullet

Such a map is called
differentiable at
\mathbf{v_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{V}}
if there exists a bounded linear map
{\rm D}f(v_{0}):V\to W
such that
\|f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-{\rm D}f(v_{0})v\|^{W}/\|v\|^{V}\to 0
as
\|v\|^{V}\to 0
, or equivalently

\forall\varepsilon>0\;\exists\delta>0:\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(v\in V,\|v\|^{V}\leq\delta\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-{\rm D}f(v_{0})v\|^{W}\leq\varepsilon\|v\|^{V}\bigr).

If
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
is differentiable at
v_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{V}
, then this condition determines the linear map
{\rm D}f(v_{0}):V\to W
uniquely, and it is called the
differential of
\mathbf{f}
at
\mathbf{v_{0}}
.

\bullet

Further,
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
is called
of class
\mathbf{\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}^{1}
if it is differentiable at all
v_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{V}
and the differential
{\rm D}f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to\mathcal{L}(V,W),v_{0}\mapsto{\rm D}f(v_{0})
is a map of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
, that is, a continuous map to the space of bounded linear operators.

\bullet

Now we can then iteratively define the notion of a
map of class
\mathbf{\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}^{\ell}
for all integers
\ell\geq 2
, by calling
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W

of class
\mathbf{\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}^{\ell}
if
{\rm D}f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to\mathcal{L}(V,W)
is of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{\ell-1}
. Equivalently,
f
is of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{\ell-1}
and the
(\ell-1)
-fold differential
{\rm D}\ldots{\rm D}f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to\mathcal{L}(V,\ldots,V,\mathcal{L}(V,W)\ldots)
is of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
.

\bullet

This definition views
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
as a vector space with norm
\|\Phi\|^{\mathcal{L}(V,W)}=\sup_{\|v\|^{V}\leq 1}\|\Phi(v)\|^{W}
, and results in higher derivatives
{\rm D}{\rm D}f(v_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(V,\mathcal{L}(V,W))
,
{\rm D}{\rm D}{\rm D}f(v_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(V,\mathcal{L}(V,\mathcal{L}(V,W)))
, etc.. These spaces are “not easy to handle”, so as in
[
Col12
, §4.3–4]
we will add to the notion of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{\ell}
an identification of the
\ell
-fold differential
{\rm D}\ldots{\rm D}f(v_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(V,\ldots,\mathcal{L}(V,W)\ldots)
with the
\ell
-th differential

{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})\in\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)
, viewed as an
\ell
-linear map
V^{\ell}\to W
. Then we can say that
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
is
of class
\mathbf{\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}^{\ell}
if it is of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{\ell-1}
and
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(V^{\ell-1};W)
is of class
\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
.

\bullet

Here
\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)
denotes the space of maps
\Phi:V^{\ell}\to W
that are
\ell
-linear, i.e. linear in each of the
\ell
entries,
\Phi(r_{1}v_{1},\ldots,r_{\ell}v_{\ell})=r_{1}\ldots r_{\ell}\Phi(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})
for all
v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\in V
and
r_{1},\ldots,r_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
, and continuous, i.e. have a finite norm

(3)

\|\Phi\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)}=\sup_{\|v_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|v_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1}\|\Phi(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\|^{W}.

\bullet

Note that
\mathcal{L}^{1}(V;W)=\mathcal{L}(V,W)
, while for
\ell\geq 2
the space
\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)
of maps that are linear in each factor is quite different from the space
\mathcal{L}(V^{\ell},W)
of linear maps.
Rather, one identifies
\mathcal{L}(V,\ldots,\mathcal{L}(V,W)\ldots)
with
\ell
factors of
V
with
\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)
by iteratively identifying
\Psi\in\mathcal{L}(V,\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(V^{\ell-1}=V\times\ldots\times V,W))
with
\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)
given by
\Psi^{\prime}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1},v_{\ell})=\Psi(v_{\ell})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1})
. Here the last equation also serves to define an inverse map
\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)\to\mathcal{L}(V,\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(V^{\ell-1};W))
, and this bijection is an isometric isomorphism since

\displaystyle\|\Psi^{\prime}\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|v_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|v_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1}\|\Psi^{\prime}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\|^{W}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|v_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1}\sup_{\|v_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|v_{\ell-1}\|^{V}\leq 1}\|\Psi(v_{\ell})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1})\|^{W}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|v_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1}\|\Psi(v_{\ell})\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(V^{\ell-1};W)}\;=\;\|\Psi\|^{\mathcal{L}(V,\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(V^{\ell-1};W))}.

\bullet

In summary, a continuous map
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
if and only if for each
1\leq\ell\leq k
the
\ell
-th differential exists

{\rm D}^{\ell}f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\;\to\;\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W),\hskip 10.00002ptv_{0}\mapsto{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})

and is continuous w.r.t. the above norm on
\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V^{\ell};W)
, that is

\sup_{\|v_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|v_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1}\|{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v)(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})-{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\|^{W}\;\underset{v\to v_{0}}{\longrightarrow}\;0.

\bullet

Finally, note that – assuming
{\rm D}^{\ell}f
exists and is differentiable – we can compute
{\rm D}^{\ell+1}f
from directional derivatives

{\rm D}^{\ell+1}f(v_{0})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell},v_{\ell+1})=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}|_{t=0}{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0}+tv_{\ell+1})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}).

Thus we are prepared for the technical proof of this section – the surprisingly subtle fact that continuous extension to Banach completions preserves Fréchet differentiability classes.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma})
and
(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega})
be normed vector spaces, and suppose that
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega
is a uniformly continuous map from an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\Gamma
.
Then there is a unique continuous extension
\overline{\mathcal{F}}:\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}\to\overline{\Omega}
of
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}=\mathcal{F}
, where
\overline{\Gamma}:=\overline{\Gamma}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}}
and
\overline{\Omega}:=\overline{\Omega}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}}
are the completions and
\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}\subset\overline{\Gamma}
is the closure of
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{\Gamma}
. In fact,
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is uniformly continuous.

If, moreover,
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
for some
\ell\geq 1
, then its continuous extension
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\overline{\Omega}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
as well, where
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}:={\rm int}(\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}})
is the interior of
\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}\subset\overline{\Gamma}
. In particular,
{\rm D}^{\ell}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})
is given by the continuous extension of

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega}),\gamma\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}
, where each
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}:\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell}\to\overline{\Omega}
is the continuous extension of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}^{\ell}(\gamma):\Gamma^{\ell}\to\Omega
.

Proof.

Recall that the completion
\overline{\Gamma}=\{[(\gamma_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}]\,|\,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Gamma\;\text{Cauchy sequence}\}
is constructed from Cauchy sequences by identifying sequences
(\gamma_{i})\sim(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})
if
\|\gamma_{i}-\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\|^{\Gamma}\to 0
.
Uniform continuity guarantees that
\mathcal{F}
maps Cauchy sequences to Cauchy sequences and preserves the equivalence relation as well. So
\overline{\mathcal{F}}:[(\gamma_{i})]\mapsto[(\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}))]
is well defined for any Cauchy sequence
(\gamma_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
.
This map satisfies
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}=\mathcal{F}
since
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{\Gamma}
is contained via
\gamma\mapsto(\gamma)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. The extension to the closure
\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}
is then uniquely determined, since for any convergent sequence
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\ni\gamma_{i}\to\gamma_{\infty}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}
continuity requires
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})=\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})
. This agrees with the construction of
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
since
[(\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}))]\in\overline{\Omega}
is the limit of
(\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
w.r.t. the norm on the completion,

\|(\omega_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|^{\overline{\Omega}}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|\omega_{i}\|^{\Omega}.

Indeed,
\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{j})-[(\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}]\|^{\overline{\Omega}}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{j})-\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\|^{\Omega}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
follows from the Cauchy property of
(\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}))
.
In fact, the uniform continuity of
\mathcal{F}
, stated as

\forall\varepsilon>0\;\exists\delta>0:\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\in\Gamma,\|\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\|^{\Gamma}\leq\delta\;\Rightarrow\;\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})-\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\|^{\Omega}\leq\epsilon\bigr),

directly transfers to
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
with the same constants:

\displaystyle[(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})],[(\gamma_{i})]\in\overline{\Gamma},\|[(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})]-[(\gamma_{i})]\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\leq\delta

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;\exists I\in\mathbb{N}:\forall i\geq I\;\|\gamma^{\prime}_{i}-\gamma_{i}\|^{\Gamma}\leq\delta

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;\exists I\in\mathbb{N}:\forall i\geq I\;\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})-\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\|^{\Omega}\leq\varepsilon

\displaystyle\;\Rightarrow\;\|[(\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}_{i}))]-[(\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}))]\|^{\overline{\Omega}}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})-\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\|^{\Omega}\leq\varepsilon.

Next, we assume that
\mathcal{F}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, that is it is uniformly continuous, differentiable, and
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\Omega)
is uniformly continuous. To prove the Lemma for
\ell=1
we then need to prove that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}
is differentiable with uniformly continuous differential
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})
. We begin by constructing a candidate for this uniformly continuous family of linear operators.

Since each differential
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma):\Gamma\to\Omega
at
\gamma\in\Gamma
is linear and continuous (hence uniformly continuous), it extends to a bounded linear operator between the completions
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}:\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
with the same norm
\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}=\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\|^{\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\Omega)}
.
Then uniform continuity of the differential
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\Omega)
can be phrased as

\forall\varepsilon>0\;\exists\delta>0:\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\|^{\Gamma}\leq\delta\;\Rightarrow\;\forall\eta\in\Gamma:\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})\eta-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\eta\|^{\Omega}\leq\varepsilon\|\eta\|^{\Gamma}\bigr).

By going to the completion in the conclusion this implies that
\gamma\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}
is a uniformly continuous map
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})
,

\forall\varepsilon>0\;\exists\delta>0:\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\|^{\Gamma}\leq\delta\;\Rightarrow\;\forall\eta\in\overline{\Gamma}:\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})}\eta-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}\eta\|^{\overline{\Omega}}\leq\varepsilon\|\eta\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\bigr).

Then this uniformly continuous map extends to a map on the completion
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega}),\gamma_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma_{i}\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}):=\lim_{i\to\infty}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}
with again the same uniform continuity property

\forall\varepsilon>0\;\exists\delta>0:\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}},\|\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\leq\delta\;\Rightarrow\;\forall\eta\in\overline{\Gamma}:\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma^{\prime})\eta-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma)\eta\|^{\overline{\Omega}}\leq\varepsilon\|\eta\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\bigr).

Moreover, the operator norm of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}
at points
\gamma_{\infty}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\smallsetminus\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
is the limit of operator norms of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}
,

\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\|^{\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\Omega)}.

It remains to show that this uniformly continuous family of bounded linear operators is in fact the differential of the completed nonlinear map
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
. For that purpose we estimate for any
\gamma_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
and
\eta_{\infty}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\eta_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle\bigl\|\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})-\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\eta_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\bigl\|\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{i}+\eta_{\infty})-\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{i})-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}\eta_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\eta_{j})-\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\eta_{j}\bigr\|^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl\|\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{j})\eta_{j}{\rm d}\lambda-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\eta_{j}\bigr\|^{\Omega}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl(\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{j})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\Omega)}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{j}\|^{\Gamma}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl(\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{j})}-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{j}\|^{\Gamma}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}.

Now for
\eta_{\infty}\neq 0
this estimate can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bigl\|\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})-\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\eta_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}\;/\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda,

where we can argue with the continuity of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}
at
\gamma_{\infty}
that the right hand side converges to zero as
\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\to 0
. This proves that
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})
is indeed the differential of
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
at
\gamma_{\infty}
. And since
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})
was constructed to be uniformly continuous, this proves that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. This proves the Lemma in case
\ell=1
with the additional fact that
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega})
is the continuous extension of
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Omega}),\gamma\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}
, where each
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}:\overline{\Gamma}\to\overline{\Omega}
is the continuous extension of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma):\Gamma\to\Omega
.

We will now extend this statement to
\ell\geq 2
by induction, assuming it is already established that for any uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}
-map
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega
between open subsets of normed vector spaces the continuous extension
\overline{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\overline{\Omega}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}
as well, with
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}}
given by the continuous extension of

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell-1};\overline{\Omega}),\gamma\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}
, where each
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}:\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell-1}\to\overline{\Omega}
is the continuous extension of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}^{\ell-1}(\gamma):\Gamma^{\ell-1}\to\Omega
.

For the induction step, we assume in addition that
\mathcal{F}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
, and aim to prove that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
as well, with
{\rm D}^{\ell}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}
.
From the induction assumption we already know that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}
with
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}}
. So it remains to prove that
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
with
{\rm D}{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}
.
The assumption that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
implies in particular that
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma})\to(\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\Gamma^{\ell-1};\Omega),\|\cdot\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\Gamma^{\ell-1};\Omega)})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, where the norm on multilinear operators
\Phi:\Gamma^{\ell-1}\to\Omega
is as in Remark
2.3

\|\Phi\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\Gamma^{\ell-1};\Omega)}=\sup\bigl\{\|\Phi(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell-1})\|^{\Omega}\,\big|\,\|\gamma_{1}\|^{\Gamma},\ldots,\|\gamma_{\ell-1}\|^{\Gamma}\leq 1\bigr\}.

The uniform
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-regularity means that there is a map
\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\Gamma^{\ell-1};\Omega)),\gamma\mapsto{\rm D}{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)
that is uniformly continuous and satisfies

\bigl\|{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma+\eta)-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)-{\rm D}{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\eta\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\Gamma^{\ell-1};\Omega))}/\|\eta\|^{\Gamma}\;\underset{\|\eta\|^{\Gamma}\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\;0.

Here we follow Remark
2.3
to identify
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\Gamma^{\ell-1};\Omega))\simeq\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\Gamma^{\ell};\Omega)
and call the resulting operator
{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma):={\rm D}{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\in\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\Gamma^{\ell};\Omega)
the
\ell
-th derivative of
\mathcal{F}
at
\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
. Since each
{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma):\Gamma^{\ell}\to\Omega
is uniformly continuous, it extends to a uniformly continuous map between the completions
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}:\overline{\Gamma^{\ell}}=\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell}\to\overline{\Omega}
with the same multilinearity property (by continuity) and the same norm
\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})}=\|{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\Gamma^{\ell};\Omega)}
. Next, as in the
\ell=1
case above, the fact that the operators
{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\in\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\Gamma^{\ell};\Omega)
vary uniformly continuously with
\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
implies uniform continuity of the continuous extension
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega}),\gamma\mapsto\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma)}
. Finally, we claim that this uniformly continuous family of operators – viewed as a map
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma},\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell-1};\overline{\Omega}))
– is the differential of
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
. Once established, this means that
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and thus
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
, as claimed.

To show that
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}
is in fact the differential of
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
we estimate for any
\gamma_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
,
\eta_{\infty}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\eta_{j}\in\overline{\Gamma}
such that
\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
, and
\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}=(\kappa_{n,\infty})_{n=1,\ldots,\ell-1}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\underline{\kappa}_{j}=(\kappa_{n,j})_{n=1,\ldots,\ell-1}\in\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl({\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\eta_{\infty}\bigr)\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})(\eta_{\infty},\underline{\kappa}_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{i}+\eta_{\infty})\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}(\eta_{\infty},\underline{\kappa}_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\eta_{j})\underline{\kappa}_{j}-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\underline{\kappa}_{j}-{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})(\eta_{j},\underline{\kappa}_{j})\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\eta_{j})\underline{\kappa}_{j}-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\underline{\kappa}_{j}-{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})(\eta_{j},\underline{\kappa}_{j})\bigr\|^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl\|\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{j})(\eta_{j},\underline{\kappa}_{j}){\rm d}\lambda-{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})(\eta_{j},\underline{\kappa}_{j})\bigr\|^{\Omega}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl(\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{j})-{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\Gamma^{\ell};\Omega)}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{j}\|^{\Gamma}\prod_{n=1}^{\ell-1}\|\kappa_{n,j}\|^{\Gamma}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl(\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{j})}-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}(\gamma_{i})}\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{j}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\prod_{n=1}^{\ell-1}\|\kappa_{n,j}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{i}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{i})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\prod_{n=1}^{\ell-1}\|\kappa_{n,\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\prod_{n=1}^{\ell-1}\|\kappa_{n,\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}.

In the operator norm (
3
) on
(\ell-1)
-linear maps, this implies

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\eta_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell-1};\overline{\Omega})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\kappa_{1,\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}},\ldots,\|\kappa_{\ell-1,\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\leq 1}\bigl\|\bigl({\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\eta_{\infty}\bigr)\underline{\kappa}_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\overline{\Omega}}
