\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}},

which we can rewrite for
\eta_{\infty}\neq 0
as

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\eta_{\infty})-{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\eta_{\infty}\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell-1}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell-1};\overline{\Omega})}\;/\;\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty}+\lambda\eta_{\infty})-\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\ell};\overline{\Omega})}{\rm d}\lambda.

Here we can argue with the continuity of
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}
at
\gamma_{\infty}
that the right hand side converges to zero as
\|\eta_{\infty}\|^{\overline{\Gamma}}\to 0
.
This proves that
\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}(\gamma_{\infty})
is indeed the differential of
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}
at
\gamma_{\infty}
, and thus
\overline{\mathcal{F}}|_{\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
with
{\rm D}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}}
, as claimed.
∎

Finally, the notions of adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity with respect to
\Delta
for
\ell\geq 2
will be defined in terms of tangent maps, rather than differentials, which we define in analogy to
[
HWZ21
, Def.1.1.14-15]
.
9
9

9

Note, however, that we are not implementing sc-calculus in this paper, so our definitions agree with
[
HWZ21
]
only in the case of finite dimensional normed spaces.

Definition 2.5
.

For any open subset
\mathcal{V}_{V}\subset V
of a topological vector space we define the
tangent space

{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{V}:=\mathcal{V}_{V}\times V
as a subset of
V^{2}=V\times V={\rm T}V
.
For
\ell\geq 2
we define
higher tangent spaces
iteratively by
{\rm T}^{\ell+1}\mathcal{V}_{V}:={\rm T}[{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}]
. Equivalently, we have

\displaystyle{\rm T}^{\ell+1}\mathcal{V}_{V}

\displaystyle:=\;{\rm T}[{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}]\;=\;{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}\times\text{ambient vector space of ${\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}$}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=\;{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}\times{\rm T}^{\ell}V\;=\;\mathcal{V}_{V}\times V\times\ldots\times V\;=\;\mathcal{V}_{V}\times V^{N_{\ell}}

\displaystyle\;\subset\;{\rm T}^{\ell}V\times{\rm T}^{\ell}V\;=\;{\rm T}^{\ell+1}V\;=\;V^{N_{\ell}+1}

with
N_{\ell}:=2^{\ell}-1
.
We moreover denote
{\rm T}^{0}\mathcal{V}_{V}:=\mathcal{V}_{V}
for efficiency of notation.

When
V
is equipped with a norm
\|\cdot\|^{V}
, then we equip higher tangent spaces
{\rm T}^{\ell}V
with the norm

\|(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}V}:=\|v_{0}\|^{V}+\ldots+\|v_{N_{\ell}}\|^{V}.

The entries of a higher tangent vector
\underline{v}=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})
are referred to as
base point

v_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{V}
and
vector entries

v_{1},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}}\in V
.
10
10

10

This differentiation arises from the different roles of base point and vector entries in the following notion of higher tangent maps.
The vector entries
v_{i\geq 1}
are always unbounded and can be multiplied by any scalar – with linearity properties in the higher differentials making up the higher tangent maps. The base point
v_{0}
is usually restricted to a bounded domain and – even if it was scaleable – has linearity properties only when studying a linear map.

We denote the
higher tangent fibers
– the subsets of higher tangent vectors with fixed base point – by

{\rm T}_{v}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}\,:=\;\bigl\{\underline{v}=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}\,\big|\,v_{0}=v\bigr\}\;\simeq\;\bigl\{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})\in V^{N_{\ell}}\bigr\}

and equip them with the
fiber norm
11
11

11

The choice of this norm simplifies estimates of higher differentials in the space of multilinear maps.
It is, of course, equivalent to the restriction of
\|\cdot\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}V}
to the fiber – but by constants depending on
\ell
.

(4)

\|(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}_{\bullet}V}\,:=\;\max\bigl\{\|v_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|v_{N_{\ell}}\|^{V}\bigr\}.

Now consider normed vector spaces
(V,\|\cdot\|^{V})
and
(W,\|\cdot\|^{W})
and a differentiable map
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
defined on an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{V}\subset V
.
We compile the map and its differential
{\rm D}f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to\mathcal{L}(V,W)
(defined in Remark
2.3
) into the
tangent map

{\rm T}f\,:\;{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{V}=\mathcal{V}_{V}\times V\;\to\;{\rm T}W=W\times W,\hskip 20.00003pt(v_{0},v)\;\mapsto\;\bigl(f(v_{0}),{\rm D}f(v_{0})v\bigr).

Then we define higher tangent maps iteratively by
{\rm T}^{\ell+1}f:={\rm T}[{\rm T}^{\ell}f]
for
\ell\geq 2
, that is
{\rm T}^{\ell+1}f(\underline{v},\underline{v}^{\prime})=({\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v}),{\rm D}[{\rm T}^{\ell}f](\underline{v})\underline{v}^{\prime})
for
(\underline{v},\underline{v}^{\prime})\in{\rm T}^{\ell+1}\mathcal{V}_{V}={\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}\times{\rm T}^{\ell}V
. We denote
{\rm T}^{0}f:=f
for efficient notation.

Remark 2.6
.

\bullet

The advantage of the tangent map notation is that it keeps track of the base point of the tangent vectors, so that the notationally tricky chain rule for differentials
{\rm D}(f\circ g)(p)={\rm D}f(g(p))\circ{\rm D}g(p)
simplifies to the chain rule for tangent maps as in
[
HWZ21
, Thm.1.3.1.]
:

If
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
and
g:\mathcal{V}_{U}\to V
are differentiable with
g(\mathcal{V}_{U})\subset\mathcal{V}_{V}
, then

(5)

{\rm T}(f\circ g)={\rm T}f\circ{\rm T}g.

\bullet

The higher tangent maps are made up of – but algebraically quite different from – higher differentials.
This difference is already notable for a linear map
F:V\to W
, whose differential is
{\rm D}F(v_{0})=F
for any base point
v_{0}\in V
, and thus all higher differentials vanish
{\rm D}^{\ell\geq 2}F=0
. In contrast, the tangent map is of the form
{\rm T}F(v_{0},v_{1})=\bigl(F(v_{0}),F(v_{1})\bigr)
, and since higher tangent maps arise from differentiation in all variables – rather than just in the base point
v_{0}
– they are given by the linear map in each component. That is, for all
\ell\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle{\rm T}^{\ell}F\,:\;{\rm T}^{\ell}V=V\times\ldots\times V

\displaystyle\;\to\;{\rm T}^{\ell}W=W\times\ldots\times W,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt(v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\bigl(F(v_{0}),F(v_{1}),\ldots,F(v_{N_{\ell}})\bigr).

Indeed, arguing by induction, the
i
-th component of
{\rm D}[{\rm T}^{\ell}F](\underline{v})\underline{v}^{\prime}
is
\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}|_{t=0}\bigl(F(v_{i}+tv^{\prime}_{i})\bigr)=F(v^{\prime}_{i})
.

\bullet

For a general – sufficiently differentiable – map
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
we can express higher tangent maps
{\rm T}^{\ell}f
in terms of higher differentials as follows: For
\ell=2
we have

\displaystyle{\rm T}^{2}f\,:\;{\rm T}^{2}\mathcal{V}_{V}=\mathcal{V}_{V}\times V\times V\times V

\displaystyle\;\to\;{\rm T}^{2}W=W\times W\times W\times W,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\bigl(f(v_{0}),{\rm D}f(v_{0})v_{1},{\rm D}f(v_{0})v_{2},{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v_{2})+{\rm D}f(v_{0})v_{3}\bigr),

where we can recover
{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v_{2})
as the last entry of
{\rm T}^{2}f(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},0)
.

Then
{\rm T}^{3}f\,:\;{\rm T}^{3}\mathcal{V}_{V}={\rm T}^{2}\mathcal{V}_{V}\times{\rm T}^{2}V\;\to\;{\rm T}^{3}W={\rm T}^{2}W\times{\rm T}^{2}W
is

(\underline{v},\underline{v}^{\prime})\mapsto\bigl({\rm T}^{2}f(\underline{v}),{\rm D}{\rm T}^{2}f(\underline{v})\underline{v}^{\prime}\bigr)
where

{\rm D}{\rm T}^{2}f(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})(v_{4}=v^{\prime}_{0},v_{5}=v^{\prime}_{1},v_{6}=v^{\prime}_{2},v_{7}=v^{\prime}_{3})\;=\;(w^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{2},w^{\prime}_{3})
consists of

\displaystyle w^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}|_{t=0}\bigl(f(v_{0}+tv^{\prime}_{0})\bigr)\;=\;{\rm D}f(v_{0})v^{\prime}_{0},

\displaystyle w^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}|_{t=0}\bigl({\rm D}f(v_{0}+tv^{\prime}_{0})(v_{1}+tv^{\prime}_{1})\bigr)\;=\;{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v_{0}^{\prime})+{\rm D}f(v_{0})v^{\prime}_{1},

\displaystyle w^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}|_{t=0}\bigl({\rm D}f(v_{0}+tv^{\prime}_{0})(v_{2}+tv^{\prime}_{2})\bigr)\;=\;{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{2},v_{0}^{\prime})+{\rm D}f(v_{0})v^{\prime}_{2},

\displaystyle w^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}|_{t=0}\bigl({\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0}+tv^{\prime}_{0})(v_{1}+tv^{\prime}_{1},v_{2}+tv^{\prime}_{2})+{\rm D}f(v_{0}+tv^{\prime}_{0})(v_{3}+tv^{\prime}_{3})\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle={\rm D}^{3}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v_{2},v^{\prime}_{0})+{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v^{\prime}_{1},v_{2})+{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v^{\prime}_{2})+{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{3},v^{\prime}_{0})+{\rm D}f(v_{0})v^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle={\rm D}^{3}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v_{2},v_{4})+{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{5},v_{2})+{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{1},v_{6})+{\rm D}^{2}f(v_{0})(v_{3},v_{4})+{\rm D}f(v_{0})v_{7}.

Inductively, we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm T}^{\ell}f\,:\;{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}=\mathcal{V}_{V}\times\ldots\times V

\displaystyle\;\to\;{\rm T}^{\ell}W=W\times\cdots\times W,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N_{\ell}})

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;\bigl(f(v_{0}),\cdots,{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2^{\ell-1}})+\cdots+{\rm D}f(v_{0})v_{2^{\ell}-1}\bigr),

where all other terms
\cdots
are sums of differentials
{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})(v_{*},\ldots,v_{*})
of order
{1\leq k\leq\ell-1}
with
(v_{*},\ldots,v_{*})
some permutation of a subset of
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2^{\ell}-1})
.

\bullet

In particular, we can recover the
\ell
-th differential
{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2^{\ell-1}})
as the last entry of
{\rm T}^{\ell}f(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},0,v_{4},0,\ldots,v_{2^{\ell-1}},0,\ldots,0)
.
Recall here that the
k
-th differential
{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\in\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{\ell};W)
is a
k
-linear map
V^{k}\to W
as defined in Remark
2.6

However, the different summands in each component of
{\rm T}^{\ell}f
have different numbers of arguments, thus
{\rm T}^{\ell}f
has no evident linearity properties (unless
f
itself is linear).

The simplification of the chain rule in the tangent map notation is counterbalanced by algebraic complications in the relationship between higher differentials and higher tangent maps – which we need to analyze in order to transfer analytic bounds between them.
As seen in the above remark, a higher differential
{\rm D}^{\ell}f
can be read off from the last component of the corresponding higher tangent map
{\rm T}^{\ell}f
, evaluated on specific higher tangent vectors. Thus we can transfer uniform bounds at a base point
v_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{W}
by

(6)

\displaystyle\|{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V,W)}

\displaystyle=\sup\bigl\{\|{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v_{0})(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})\|^{W}\,\big|\,\|v_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|v_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\bigl\{\|{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}_{\bullet}W}\,\big|\,\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}V}\leq 1\bigr\}.

Transfering continuity estimates already becomes more tricky, as we need to compare derivatives in the same directions. We can estimate this for
u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{W}
by

(7)

\displaystyle\|{\rm D}^{\ell}f(u)-{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v)\|^{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}(V,W)}

\displaystyle=\sup\bigl\{\|{\rm D}^{\ell}f(u)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})-{\rm D}^{\ell}f(v)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\|^{W}\,\big|\,\|x_{1}\|^{V},\ldots,\|x_{\ell}\|^{V}\leq 1\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\bigl\{\|{\rm T}^{\ell}f((u,0,\ldots,0)+\underline{x})-{\rm T}^{\ell}f((v,0,\ldots,0)+\underline{x})\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}_{\bullet}W}\,\big|\,\underline{x}\in{\rm T}_{0}V,\|\underline{x}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}V}\leq 1\bigr\}.

Bounding higher tangent maps in terms of higher differentials is more complicated.
The following estimate is adapted to our application needs. It also inductively bounds
{\rm T}^{\ell+1}f=({\rm T}^{\ell}f,{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f)
.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exist constants
C^{\ell}_{{\rm T}}
for all
\ell\geq 0
such that, given any

\ell
-fold differentiable map
f:\mathcal{V}_{V}\to W
between normed vector spaces

(V,\|\cdot\|^{V})
and
(W,\|\cdot\|^{W})
defined on an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{V}\subset V
,
we have for all
\underline{v}=(v_{0},\ldots)\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}({\rm T}^{\ell}V,{\rm T}^{\ell}W)}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\ell}_{{\rm T}}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}(\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\})^{k},

and for all
\underline{u}=(u_{0},\ldots),\underline{v}=(v_{0},\ldots)\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{u})-{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}({\rm T}^{\ell}V,{\rm T}^{\ell}W)}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\leq C^{\ell}_{{\rm T}}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\Bigl(\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(u_{0})-{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}\max\{\|\underline{u}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V},\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\}^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}\|\underline{u}-\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\max\{\|\underline{u}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V},\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\}^{k-1}\Bigr).

Proof.

For
\underline{v}=(v^{0},v^{1},\ldots,v^{N_{\ell}})\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}
we have from Remark
2.6

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}({\rm T}^{\ell}V,{\rm T}^{\ell}W)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}V}\leq 1}\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\underline{x}\bigr\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}W}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1}\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\Bigl\|\textstyle\sum_{*}{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(v_{*}\ldots v_{*},x_{*},v_{*}\ldots v_{*})\Bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1}\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\textstyle\sum_{*}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}\|x_{*}\|^{V}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\textstyle\sum_{*}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\ell}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}(\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\})^{k},

where
C^{\ell}
is a universal constant determined by the combinatorics of applying the chain rule to express higher tangent maps in terms of higher differentials, which we will replace below by
C^{\ell}\leq C^{\ell}_{{\rm T}}
.

Similarly, for
\underline{u}=(u^{0},u^{1},\ldots,u^{N_{\ell}}),\underline{v}=(v^{0},v^{1},\ldots,v^{N_{\ell}})\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{V}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{u})-{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}({\rm T}^{\ell}V,{\rm T}^{\ell}W)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}V}\leq 1}\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{u})\underline{x}-{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell}f(\underline{v})\underline{x}\bigr\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}W}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1}\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\Bigl\|\textstyle\sum_{*}{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(u_{0})(u_{*}\ldots u_{*},x_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*})-{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(v_{*}\ldots v_{*},x_{*},v_{*}\ldots v_{*})\Bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1}\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\textstyle\sum_{*}\Bigl(\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(u_{0})(u_{*}\ldots u_{*},x_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*})-{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(u_{*}\ldots u_{*},x_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*})\bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(u_{*}-v_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*},x_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*})\bigr\|^{W}+\ldots

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(v_{*}\ldots v_{*},u_{*}-v_{*},x_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*})\bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(v_{*}\ldots v_{*},x_{*},u_{*}-v_{*},u_{*}\ldots u_{*})\bigr\|^{W}+\ldots

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})(v_{*}\ldots v_{*},x_{*},v_{*}\ldots v_{*},u_{*}-v_{*})\bigr\|^{W}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1}\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\textstyle\sum_{*}\Bigl(\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(u_{0})-{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}\|x_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|u_{*}-v_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}\|x_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}+\ldots

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}-v_{*}\|^{V}\|x_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}\|x_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}-v_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}+\ldots

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}\|x_{*}\|^{V}\|v_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|v_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}-v_{*}\|^{V}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\ell}}\textstyle\sum_{*}\Bigl(\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(u_{0})-{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}\|u_{*}\|^{V}\ldots\|u_{*}\|^{V}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{k_{*}}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k_{*}}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k_{*}}(V^{k_{*}},W)}\|u_{j}-v_{j}\|^{V}\max\{\|u_{*}\|^{V},\|v_{*}\|^{V}\}\ldots\max\{\|u_{*}\|^{V},\|v_{*}\|^{V}\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\ell}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(u_{0})-{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}(\|\underline{u}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V})^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+C^{\ell}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell+1}k\,\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}\|\underline{u}-\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\max\bigl\{\|\underline{u}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V},\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\bigr\}^{k-1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\ell}_{{\rm T}}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\Bigl(\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(u_{0})-{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}\max\bigl\{\|\underline{u}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V},\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\bigr\}^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}f(v_{0})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(V^{k},W)}\|\underline{u}-\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\max\bigl\{\|\underline{u}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V},\|\underline{v}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}V}\bigr\}^{k-1}\Bigr)

with
C^{\ell}_{{\rm T}}:=(\ell+1)C^{\ell}
.
∎

2.2.
Regularity Properties

This section defines various properties of adiabatic Fredholm families that will be needed to guarantee that their finite dimensional reductions are continuous and/or differentiable in the sense defined in Corollary
4.12
.
The following notion is necessary for all such regularity proofs, including continuity.
It encodes elliptic regularity of the PDEs – more specifically the fact that any solution of the inhomogeneous PDE with a smooth right hand side is itself smooth. This is also a corollary of the regularizing notion in
[
HWZ21
, Def.3.1.16]
.

Definition 2.8
.

An adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
as in Definition
2.1
is called
regularizing
if for all
\epsilon\in\Delta
any solution
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
for the extended adiabatic Fredholm family of a nonlinear equation
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=\omega
or a linear equation
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma=\omega
at
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
, whose right hand side lies in the
\epsilon
-independent dense target subspace
\omega\in\Omega\subset\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
, is guaranteed to lie in the
\epsilon
-independent dense domain subspace
\gamma\in\Gamma\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
.
More precisely, we have the two implications

(8)

\displaystyle\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon},\;\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\in\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},

(9)

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\;\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\;{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma\in\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\in\Gamma.

Remark 2.9
.

Using the tangent map notation from Definition
2.5
, the regularizing property can equivalent be phrased as

(10)

\underline{\gamma}\in{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon},\;{\rm T}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma})\in{\rm T}\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\underline{\gamma}\in{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}.

Indeed, our definitions yield
{\rm T}\Omega=\Omega\times\Omega
as well as

{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\times\Gamma

and

{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}=\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\times\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon
.
Now for
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\times\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon
we have

{\rm T}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma})=\bigl(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0}),{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}\bigr)
.
So if (
10
) holds, then applying it to
(\gamma_{0},0)
yields (
8
), whereas applying it to

(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\times\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon
yields (
9
).
Conversely, (
8
) and (
9
) imply (
10
)
since the first component gives
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\in\Omega
, which by (
8
) implies
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
. Then the second component is
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}\in\Omega
and (
9
) applies to yield
\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
.

Thus it makes sense to define the regularizing property for higher tangent maps
{\rm T}^{\ell}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}
as well – which is done in (
11
) as a part of the adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity.

The next notion encodes differentiability for fixed
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

Definition 2.10
.

Given
\ell\geq 2
, an adiabatic Fredholm family as in Definition
2.1
is called
fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular
if it satisfies

[Fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
Regularity]

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
.

Next, we will use the language of tangent maps from Definition
2.5
to formulate the notions of (higher) tangent maps of an adiabatic Fredholm family being continuous with respect to
\Delta
. Formulating these is complicated by the fact that we have not imposed strong enough conditions to guarantee the existence of a topology on
\Delta\times\Gamma
that restricts on each “fiber”
\{\epsilon\}\times\Gamma
to the topology induced by the norm
\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
. Instead, we will work with two types of continuity: Pointwise continuity in
\Delta
for a fixed
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
and uniform continuity in
\Gamma
whose uniformity is independent of
\epsilon\in\Delta
. These are matters where it is not a priori clear that there are notions that are both weak enough to be satisfied in examples of adiabatic limits and strong enough to provide the desired regularity of finite dimensional reductions.

The following notion of pointwise continuity generalizes the [Near Solution] and [Continuity of Derivatives at
0
] properties of Definition
2.1
– equivalent to
\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)-\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\underset{\epsilon\to 0}{\to}0
and

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\overline{\Omega}_{0})}\underset{\epsilon\to 0}{\to}0
–
to limits
\Delta\ni\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}\neq 0
, base points points
\gamma_{0}\neq 0
, and higher tangent maps. However, this is actually not true in the Examples
1.1
unless
\gamma_{0}
is sufficiently regular and lies in the subset
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
from the classical adiabatic formulation in the introduction. We can enforce this by restricting the pointwise continuity requirement to
\gamma_{0}
that solve
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\in\mathfrak{C}
– for appropriate representations of the cokernel
\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
that naturally exist in the examples.

Definition 2.11
.

An adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
of Definition
2.1
is called

•

pointwise continuous in
\mathbf{\Delta}
at solutions modulo
\mathbf{\mathfrak{C}}
(short:
continuous in
\mathbf{\Delta}
rel.
\mathbf{\mathfrak{C}}
) if, given any
\epsilon_{0}\in\Delta
and a solution
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have

\bigl\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})-\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}
;

•

pointwise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-continuous in
\mathbf{\Delta}
at solutions modulo
\mathbf{\mathfrak{C}}
(short:
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-continuous in
\mathbf{\Delta}
rel.
\mathbf{\mathfrak{C}}
) for a given
\ell\geq 0
if
it is fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
as in Definition
2.10
and the family
({\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in\Delta}
is
continuous in
\Delta
rel.
\mathfrak{C}
, that is
given any
\epsilon_{0}\in\Delta
and a solution
\underline{\gamma}_{0}\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
of
{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\underline{\gamma}_{0})\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathfrak{C}
we have

\bigl\|{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma}_{0})-{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\underline{\gamma}_{0})\bigr\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}\Omega}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}
.

Recall here that fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity is built into the notion of adiabatic Fredholm family.

Remark 2.12
.

Note that pointwise continuity in
\Delta
rel.
\mathfrak{C}
is pointwise
\mathcal{C}^{0}
-continuity in
\Delta
rel.
\mathfrak{C}
.

For
\ell=1
recall that
{\rm T}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})=(\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0}),{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1})
for
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in{\rm T}\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\times\Gamma
. So an adiabatic Fredholm family is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
in
\Delta
rel.
\mathfrak{C}
if and only if it is continuous in
\Delta
rel.
\mathfrak{C}
and so is its differential:

•

Given any
\epsilon_{0}\in\Delta
and solutions
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\in\mathfrak{C}
and

\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma
of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\gamma_{0})\gamma_{1}\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}
.

However, for
\ell\geq 2
there is no evident characterization of
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-continuity in
\Delta
rel.
\mathfrak{C}
in terms of the higher differentials – since the components of
{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
are sums of higher differentials.

Next, the following notion of uniform continuity generalizes the [Quadratic-ish Estimate] in Definition
2.1
– equivalent to
\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\leq c(\|\gamma_{0}-0\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})
–
to any pair of base points points
\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}},\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
and higher tangent maps.
Then we package pointwise and uniform continuity into notions of adiabatic regularity as follows:

Definition 2.13
.

Given
\ell\geq 0
, an adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
of Definition
2.1
is called
adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular
– or an
adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular Fredholm family
– if it satisfies the following:

\bullet

The family is fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular as in Definition
2.10
.

\bullet

The family is regularizing as in Definition
2.8
, and in case
\ell\geq 2
so is the
\ell
-th tangent map in the sense that

(11)

\underline{\gamma}\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon},\;{\rm T}^{\ell}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma})\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\underline{\gamma}\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}.

\bullet

The family is pointwise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-continuous in
\Delta
at solutions modulo
\mathbf{\mathfrak{C}}
as in Definition
2.11
.

\bullet

[Uniform Continuity of
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
for
\mathbf{1\leq k\leq\ell\,}
]

In case
\ell=0
there is no further condition. In case
\ell\geq 1
we require monotone continuous functions
c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(0)=0

for
1\leq k\leq\ell
so that for all
\epsilon\in\Delta
and
\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}},\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
we have

\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}})-{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\leq c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}}-\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}).

\bullet

[Uniform Bound on
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)
for
\mathbf{1\leq k\leq\ell\,}
]

In case
\ell=0
there is no further condition. In case
\ell\geq 1
we require constants
C^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}\geq 1
for
1\leq k\leq\ell
so that for all
\epsilon\in\Delta
we have

(12)

\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\leq C^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\epsilon\in\Delta,\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}.

An adiabatic Fredholm family is called
adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-regular
– or an
adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-regular Fredholm family
– if it is adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 2.14
.

If an adiabatic Fredholm family satisfies
[Uniform Continuity of
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell\,
]
and
[Uniform Bound on
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)
for
1\leq k\leq\ell\,
]
for some
\ell\geq 1
, then the uniform continuity transfers to the higher tangent maps of Definition
2.5
and the extended adiabatic Fredholm family of Lemma
2.2
:

[Uniform Continuity of
{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}
]

Given any
\delta>0
there is a monotone continuous function

c^{\ell,\delta}_{{\rm T}\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
c^{\ell,\delta}_{{\rm T}\mathcal{F}}(0)=0
so that
for all
\epsilon\in\Delta
,
and
\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}},\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\in{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}
with

\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}}_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq\delta

we have

\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}})-{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}({\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\leq c^{\ell,\delta}_{{\rm T}\mathcal{F}}(\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}-\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\,\max\bigl\{1,\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\bigr\}^{\ell}.

In fact, this estimate for the extended adiabatic Fredholm family follows by continuous extension from the analogous estimate for the adiabatic Fredholm family
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
since Lemma
2.4
identifies
{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}
with the continuous extension of
{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
.

In case
\ell=1
we have
{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}={\rm D}^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
so that uniform continuity holds with

c^{1}_{{\rm T}\mathcal{F}}:=c^{1}_{\mathcal{F}}
and the uniform bound assumptions on
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
and
\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
are not needed.
Uniform continuity of
{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
for
\ell\geq 2
does require both assumptions.
To check it we first combine [Uniform Continuity of
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
and Uniform Bound on
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)
] for
1\leq k\leq\ell

to bound
{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
at
\gamma\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)-{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}

(13)

\displaystyle\leq C^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}+c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\;\leq\;C^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}+c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta)\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\;\gamma\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\;\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq\delta.

Now we can combine the assumptions with Lemma
2.7
to estimate for any given
\delta>0
and for all
\epsilon\in\Delta
and

\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}=(\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}}_{0}\ldots),\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}=(\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}_{0}\ldots)\in{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}

with
\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}}_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}_{0}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq\delta

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}})-{\rm D}{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}({\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\ell-1}_{{\rm T}}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\Bigl(\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0}^{\mathfrak{l}})-{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0}^{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\max\bigl\{\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\bigr\}^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl\|{\rm D}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{0}^{\mathfrak{l}})\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}^{k}(\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}^{k},\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon})}\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}-\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\max\bigl\{\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\bigr\}^{k-1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\ell-1}_{{\rm T}}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\Bigl(c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\gamma^{\mathfrak{l}}-\gamma^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\max\bigl\{\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\bigr\}^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\bigl(C^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}+c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta)\bigr)\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}-\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\max\bigl\{\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\bigr\}^{k-1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq c^{\ell,\delta}_{{\rm T}\mathcal{F}}(\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}-\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\,\max\bigl\{1,\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{l}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\underline{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell-1}\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\bigr\}^{\ell}

with
c^{\ell,\delta}_{{\rm T}\mathcal{F}}(x):=C^{\ell-1}_{{\rm T}}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}(c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)+C^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}\,x+c^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(\delta)\,x)
.

3.
Examples

This section sketches how Examples
1.1
[QF] and [AF] can be described in terms of adiabatic Fredholm families.

3.1.
Quilted Floer Theory and Geometric Composition of Lagrangians

Consider Lagrangian correspondences
{L_{01}\subset M_{0}^{-}\times M_{1}}
and
L_{12}\subset M_{1}^{-}\times M_{2}
, where
M_{\ell}=(M_{\ell},\omega_{M_{\ell}})
are symplectic manifolds and
M_{\ell}^{-}\coloneqq(M_{\ell},-\omega_{M_{\ell}})
.
The
geometric composition
of such Lagrangian correspondences is

L_{01}\circ L_{12}\coloneqq{\rm Pr}_{02}(L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12})
, the image under the projection
{\rm Pr}_{02}\colon M_{0}^{-}\times M_{1}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{2}\to M_{0}^{-}\times M_{2}
of the fiber product

\displaystyle L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12}\coloneqq(L_{01}\times L_{12})\cap(M_{0}^{-}\times\Delta_{1}\times M_{2}).

Here
\Delta_{1}\subset M_{1}\times M_{1}^{-}
denotes the diagonal.
If
L_{01}\times L_{12}
intersects
M_{0}^{-}\times\Delta_{1}\times M_{2}
transversely then
{\rm Pr}_{02}\colon L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12}\to M_{0}^{-}\times M_{2}
is a Lagrangian immersion, in which case we call
L_{01}\circ L_{12}
an
immersed composition
.
In the case of
embedded composition
, where the projection is injective and hence a Lagrangian embedding, some strict monotonicity and Maslov index assumptions allowed
[
WW12
]
to establish an isomorphism of quilted Floer cohomologies

(14)

HF(\ldots,L_{01},L_{12},\ldots)\cong HF(\ldots,L_{01}\circ L_{12},\ldots).

This isomorphism can also be stated in terms of classical Lagrangian Floer homologies for Cartesian products. For example, it identifies

HF(L_{0}\times L_{12},L_{01}\times L_{2})\simeq HF(L_{0}\times L_{2},L_{01}\circ L_{12})

by relating Floer trajectories in
M_{0}\times M_{1}\times M_{2}
Floer trajectories in
M_{0}\times M_{2}
, as indicated in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Tuples of pseudoholomorphic strips that are related by the isomorphism

HF(L_{0}\times L_{12},L_{01}\times L_{2})\simeq HF(L_{0}\times L_{2},L_{01}\circ L_{12})

The analytic core of the proof was an adiabatic limit called “strip-shrinking”, in which a triple of pseudoholomorphic strips coupled by Lagrangian seam conditions degenerates to a pair of strips, via the width of the middle strip shrinking to zero.
Here the monotonicity and embeddedness assumptions allowed for an implicit exclusion of all bubbling, and to apply the classical adiabatic method (as sketched in the introduction) to transversely cut out moduli spaces of dimension
0
.

For general (compact or geometrically bounded) symplectic manifolds and general immersed composition
12
12

12

Unlike embeddedness, the transversality required for immersed composition can always be achieved by a small Hamiltonian perturbation of the Lagrangians.
of the Lagrangian correspondences,
[
BW18
]
establishes a Gromov compactness theorem for strip shrinking – including a full geometric understanding of all bubbling. The algebraic impact of bubbling was then cast into the first author’s proposal of the symplectic
(A_{\infty},2)
-category
[
AB24
]
. So the foundational piece missing for a systematic description of the functorial properties of Fukaya categories is a local finite dimensional description for strip-shrinking moduli spaces, which is now provided by Theorem
1.8
– coupled with gluing constructions as outlined in Remark
1.6
– whenever the moduli space is described as the zero set of an adiabatic Fredholm family.

In the following we develop this adiabatic Fredholm description for the archetypical sample case of three Floer strips coupled by Lagrangians
L_{01},L_{12}
, with the middle strip of width
\epsilon>0
being replaced in the
\epsilon=0
limit by coupling the two remaining strips via the immersed Lagrangian
L_{01}\circ L_{12}
.
For that purpose we fix the following data:

\bullet

(M_{0},\omega_{0},J_{0}),(M_{1},\omega_{1},J_{1}),(M_{2},\omega_{2},J_{2})
are symplectic manifolds equipped with compatible almost complex structures.

\bullet

L_{0}\subset M_{0}
,
L_{01}\subset M_{0}^{-}\times M_{1}
,
L_{12}\subset M_{1}^{-}\times M_{2}
,
L_{2}\subset M_{2}
are Lagrangian submanifolds satisfying

–

immersed composition
L_{01}\times L_{12}\;\pitchfork\;M_{0}^{-}\times\Delta_{1}\times M_{2}
,

–

transverse intersection
L_{0}\times L_{12}\;\pitchfork\;L_{01}\times L_{2}
.

\bullet

\underline{x}^{\pm}=(x_{0}^{\pm},x_{1}^{\pm},x_{2}^{\pm})\in L_{0}\times L_{12}\cap L_{01}\times L_{2}
are intersection points of the tuples of Lagrangians.

Then the
strip-shrinking moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{[0,1]}:=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in[0,1]}\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
is the union of the following moduli spaces for
\epsilon>0
resp.
\epsilon=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\epsilon}}:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{aligned} u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\\
u_{1}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,{\bm{\epsilon}}]\to M_{1}\\
u_{2}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{2}\end{aligned}\right)\;\right|\;\begin{aligned} \partial_{s}u_{0}+J_{0}(u_{0})\partial_{t}u_{0}=0,\\
\partial_{s}u_{1}+J_{1}(u_{1})\partial_{t}u_{1}=0,\\
\partial_{s}u_{2}+J_{2}(u_{2})\partial_{t}u_{2}=0,\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &u_{0}|_{t=0}\in L_{0},\;u_{2}|_{t=1}\in L_{2},\\
&(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{1}|_{t=0})\in L_{01},\\
&(u_{1}|_{t={\bm{\epsilon}}},u_{2}|_{t=0})\in L_{12}\end{aligned}\right\}/\;\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{0}}:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{aligned} u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\\
u_{1}:\mathbb{R}\times{\bm{\{0\}}}\to M_{1}\\
u_{2}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{2}\end{aligned}\right)\;\right|\;\begin{aligned} \partial_{s}u_{0}+J_{0}(u_{0})\partial_{t}u_{0}=0,\\
\\
\partial_{s}u_{2}+J_{2}(u_{2})\partial_{t}u_{2}=0,\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &u_{0}|_{t=0}\in L_{0},\;u_{2}|_{t=1}\in L_{2},\\
&(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{1}|_{t={\bm{0}}})\in L_{01},\\
&(u_{1}|_{t={\bm{0}}},u_{2}|_{t=0})\in L_{12}\end{aligned}\right\}/\;\mathbb{R}.

Here and throughout we require

\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\bigl(u_{0}(s,\cdot),u_{1}(s,\cdot),u_{2}(s,\cdot)\bigr)=(x_{0}^{\pm},x_{1}^{\pm},x_{2}^{\pm})
and quotient by simultaneous
\mathbb{R}
-shifts
\bigl(u_{0}(\cdot,\cdot),u_{1}(\cdot,\cdot),u_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigr)\sim\bigl(u_{0}(R+\cdot,\cdot),u_{1}(R+\cdot,\cdot),u_{2}(R+\cdot,\cdot)\bigr)
for all
R\in\mathbb{R}
.

Note here that for embedded geometric composition
L_{01}\circ L_{12}\subset M_{0}\times M_{2}^{-}
the
\epsilon=0
moduli space can be identified with a more traditional view of Floer strips in
M_{0}\times M_{2}^{-}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{0}}\simeq\left\{\left.\left(\begin{aligned} u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\\
u_{2}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{2}\end{aligned}\right)\;\right|\;\begin{aligned} \partial_{s}u_{0}+J_{0}(u_{0})\partial_{t}u_{0}=0,\\
\partial_{s}u_{2}+J_{2}(u_{2})\partial_{t}u_{2}=0,\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &u_{0}|_{t=0}\in L_{0},\;u_{2}|_{t=1}\in L_{2},\\
&(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{2}|_{t=0})\in L_{01}\circ L_{12}\end{aligned}\right\}/\;\mathbb{R},

since
u_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to M_{1}
is determined by the unique lift of
(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{2}|_{t=0}):\mathbb{R}\to{\rm Pr}_{02}(L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12})
to
(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{1},u_{1},u_{2}|_{t=0}):\mathbb{R}\to L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12}
.
And when
{\rm Pr}_{02}:L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12}\to M_{0}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{1}\times M_{2}^{-}
is an immersion, then the lift
u_{1}
is the appropriate analytic data to keep track of.

This is the perspective used for the adiabatic analysis in
[
WW12
]
.
To make the connection with the general description of adiabatic limits in the introduction, note that the moduli spaces for
\epsilon>0
can be also be identified with moduli spaces of tuples of maps
(u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}):\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\times M_{1}\times M_{2}
defined on a common strip of width
1
, solving a deformation of the Cauchy-Riemann PDE. This identification arises by rescaling
u^{\rm new}_{1}(s,t)=u^{\rm old}_{1}(s,\epsilon\,t)
, so that
\partial_{t}u^{\rm new}_{1}=\epsilon\,u^{\rm old}_{1}
results in

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\simeq\left\{\left.\left(\begin{aligned} u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\\
u_{1}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{1}\\
u_{2}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{2}\end{aligned}\right)\;\right|\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{s}u_{0}+J_{0}(u_{0})\partial_{t}u_{0}=0,\\
&\partial_{s}u_{1}+{\bm{\epsilon}}^{-1}J_{1}(u_{1})\partial_{t}u_{1}=0,\\
&\partial_{s}u_{2}+J_{2}(u_{2})\partial_{t}u_{2}=0,\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &u_{0}|_{t=0}\in L_{0},\;u_{2}|_{t=1}\in L_{2},\\
&(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{1}|_{t=0})\in L_{01},\\
&(u_{1}|_{t=1},u_{2}|_{t=0})\in L_{12}\end{aligned}\right\}/\;\mathbb{R}.

The breakthrough idea for describing this strip-shrinking moduli space by an adiabatic Fredholm family is to multiply the PDEs with
J_{0}
,
\epsilon J_{1}
, and
J_{2}
to obtain a uniform description for all
0\leq\epsilon\leq 1

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\simeq\left\{\left.\left(\begin{aligned} u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\\
u_{1}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{1}\\
u_{2}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{2}\end{aligned}\right)\;\right|\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u_{0}-J_{0}(u_{0})\partial_{s}u_{0}=0,\\
&\partial_{t}u_{1}-{\bm{\epsilon}}J_{1}(u_{1})\partial_{s}u_{1}=0,\\
&\partial_{t}u_{2}-J_{2}(u_{2})\partial_{s}u_{2}=0,\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &u_{0}|_{t=0}\in L_{0},\;u_{2}|_{t=1}\in L_{2},\\
&(u_{0}|_{t=1},u_{1}|_{t=0})\in L_{01},\\
&(u_{1}|_{t=1},u_{2}|_{t=0})\in L_{12}\end{aligned}\right\}/\;\mathbb{R}.

That is, the idea is to view the family of nonlinear differential operators

\partial_{t}-(J_{0},\epsilon J_{1},J_{2})\partial_{s}
as an adiabatic Fredholm family on a space of maps
\underline{u}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{0}\times M_{1}\times M_{2}
with Lagrangian boundary conditions, limits
\underline{x}^{\pm}
, and a suitable local slice condition for the action of
\mathbb{R}
.

In order to be able to import the estimates from
[
WW12
]
and
[
BW18
]
) we need one more reformulation of the moduli spaces as tuples of maps to
M_{02}:=M_{0}^{-}\times M_{2}
and
\hat{M}:=M_{0211}:=M_{0}\times M_{2}^{-}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{1}
, with almost complex structures
J_{02}:=(-J_{0},J_{2})
and
\hat{J}:=(J_{0},-J_{2},-J_{1},J_{1})
, which
sets up the analysis to effectively utilize the transversality of the intersection

L_{01}\times L_{12}\,\pitchfork\,M_{0}^{-}\times\Delta_{1}\times M_{2}
.
This is described in detail in
[
WW12
, Figure 4]
, resulting in

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\simeq\left\{\left(\begin{aligned} u_{02}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to M_{02}\\
\hat{u}=(\hat{u}_{02},\hat{u}_{11}):\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to\hat{M}\end{aligned}\right)\;\left|\;\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u_{02}-J_{02}(u_{02})\partial_{s}u_{02}=0,\\
&\partial_{t}\hat{u}-{\bm{\epsilon}}\,\hat{J}(\hat{u})\partial_{s}\hat{u}=0,\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &u_{02}|_{t=1}\in L_{0}\times L_{2},\\
&u_{02}|_{t=0}=\hat{u}_{02}|_{t=0},\\
&\hat{u}_{11}|_{t=0}\in\Delta_{1},\\
&\hat{u}|_{t=1}\in L_{0211}\end{aligned}\right.\right\}/\;\mathbb{R}.

Here
L_{0211}\subset\hat{M}=M_{0}\times M_{2}^{-}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{1}
denotes the Lagrangian obtained by appropriate permutation of the factors in
L_{01}\times L_{12}\subset M_{0}^{-}\times M_{1}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{2}
.
Now fix a solution
\underline{u}=\bigl(u_{02},\hat{u}=(\hat{u}_{02},\hat{u}_{11})\bigr)
for
\epsilon=0
– noting that its second component
\hat{u}=\hat{u}(s)
is independent of
t\in[0,1]
.
Then we will describe a neighbourhood of
[\underline{u}]\in\mathcal{M}_{[0,1]}
as the zero set

\textstyle\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\;\;\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\;\;\mathcal{U}\;\subset\;\bigcup_{\epsilon\in[0,1]}\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}\;=\;\mathcal{M}_{[0,1]}

of an adiabatic Fredholm family, starting with the domain space
13
13

13

Here and throughout we write
{\rm T}_{u}L
as a short hand for
u^{*}{\rm T}L
.

\displaystyle\Gamma:=\left\{\left(\begin{aligned} &\xi_{02}\\
&\hat{\xi}=(\hat{\xi}_{02},\hat{\xi}_{11})\end{aligned}\right)\in\begin{aligned} \mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\\
\times\;\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M})\end{aligned}\;\left|\;\begin{aligned} &\xi_{02}|_{t=1}\in{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=1}}(L_{0}\times L_{2}),\\
&\xi_{02}|_{t=0}=\hat{\xi}_{02}|_{t=0},\\
&\hat{\xi}_{11}|_{t=0}\in{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1},\\
&\hat{\xi}|_{t=1}\in{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}L_{0211},\end{aligned}\;\;\begin{aligned} &\text{[slice]},\\
&\text{[decay]}.\end{aligned}\right.\right\}

Here [slice] is a slicing condition such as
\xi_{02}(z_{0})\in H_{02}
at a point where
\partial_{s}u_{02}(z_{0})\neq 0
is nonzero, thus has a codimension
1
complement in
{\rm T}_{u_{02}(z_{0})}=\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}u_{02}(z_{0})\oplus H_{02}
.
And [decay] is an exponential decay condition such as

e^{\delta|R|}\|\xi_{02}|_{[R,R+1]\times[0,1]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}+\|\hat{\xi}|_{[R,R+1]\times[0,1]}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}\to 0
as
R\to\pm\infty
with some
\delta>0
that is automatic for solutions of the PDEs (which requires the uniform exponential decay of
[
WW12
, Lemma 3.2.3]

) and will ensure that we can equip
\Gamma
with well-defined norms.

We specify a convex
\mathcal{C}^{0}
-open neighbourhood of
0=0_{\Gamma}\in\Gamma
by restricting to the injectivity radius of exponential maps
e_{02}:{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02}\to M_{02}
and
\hat{e}:{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}\to\hat{M}
,

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\,:=\;\bigl\{(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\Gamma\,\big|\,\|\xi_{02}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}},\|\hat{\xi}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}<\text{injectivity radius}\bigr\}.

The target space has no boundary or slicing conditions, just the same exponential decay condition

\displaystyle\Omega:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{aligned} &\eta_{02}\\
&\hat{\eta}\end{aligned}\right)\in\begin{aligned} \mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\\
\times\;\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M})\end{aligned}\;\right|\;\begin{aligned} &\text{[limit]}\end{aligned}\right\}.

With that we can specify the maps
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega
for
\epsilon\in\Delta:=[0,1]
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\,:=\;\bigl(\;\Phi_{02}(\xi_{02})^{-1}(\partial_{t}-J_{02}\partial_{s})\,e_{02}(\xi_{02})\;,\;\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\partial_{t}-\epsilon\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\;\bigr),

where
\Phi(\xi)
denotes the parallel transport

{\rm T}_{u}M\to{\rm T}_{e(\xi)}M
along the path
\tau\mapsto e(\tau\xi)
.
Next, we specify norms on
\Gamma
and
\Omega
for
\epsilon\in(0,1]
by pullback of the
H^{2}\cap W^{1,4}
and
H^{1}\cap L^{4}
norms that were introduced in
[
WW12
, §3.1]
to obtain uniform quadratic estimates,

\displaystyle\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon>0}

\displaystyle\,:=\;\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{2}}+\epsilon^{1/2}\bigl(\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)+\epsilon^{1/4}\bigl(\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{4}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\epsilon^{-1/2}\bigl(\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)+\epsilon^{-3/2}\|\nabla_{t}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{-3/4}\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{4}},

\displaystyle\|(\eta_{02},\hat{\eta})\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon>0}

\displaystyle\,:=\;\|\eta_{02}\|_{H^{1}}+\epsilon^{-1/2}\bigl(\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)+\epsilon^{-3/2}\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{-3/4}\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{4}}.

The completions w.r.t. these norms will be the same Banach spaces for all
\epsilon>0
– they are just equipped with
\epsilon
-dependent equivalent norms –

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon>0}

\displaystyle\;\subset\;[H^{2}\cap W^{1,4}](\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\;\times\;[H^{2}\cap W^{1,4}](\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}),

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon>0}

\displaystyle\;=\;[H^{1}\cap L^{4}](\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\;\times\;[H^{1}\cap L^{4}](\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}),

where
\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon>0}
is the closed subspace specified by the Lagrangian boundary conditions and slice condition.
For
\epsilon=0
we developed norms to obtain Fredholm properties for
\mathcal{F}_{0}
guided by the following.

Remark 3.1

(The auxiliary isomorphism for
\epsilon=0
)
.

The second component of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)
is
\hat{\xi}\mapsto\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}
, which – with the decoupled boundary conditions in
\Gamma
– induces isomorphisms for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
s\geq 0

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}\,:\;

\displaystyle\bigl\{\hat{\xi}\in W^{k+1,1}([0,1],H^{s}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))\,\big|\,\hat{\xi}_{11}|_{t=0}\in{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1},\hat{\xi}|_{t=1}\in{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}L_{0211}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\to\;W^{k,1}([0,1],H^{s}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\;\hat{\xi}=(\hat{\xi}_{02},\hat{\xi}_{11})\hskip 20.00003pt\;\mapsto\;\hskip 20.00003pt\bigl(\;\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\;,\;\pi_{02}\,\hat{\xi}_{02}|_{t=0}\;\bigr).

Indeed, its inverse is
(\hat{\eta},\lambda)\mapsto\hat{\xi}(t)={\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}^{-1}\lambda+\int_{0}^{t}\hat{\eta}(x){\rm d}x-(\pi_{02}^{\perp}\times\pi_{11})\bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\hat{\eta}(x){\rm d}x\bigr)
. This uses the isomorphism (
15
) and the projections
\pi_{02}:{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}M_{02}\to{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02}

and
\pi_{02}^{\perp}\times\pi_{11}:{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}\to({\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02})^{\perp}\times{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1}
to a complement of
{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}(M_{02}\times\Delta_{1})\cap{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}L_{0211}={\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\widetilde{L}_{02}\subset{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}(M_{02}\times\Delta_{1})
– all of which are
constructed in the following remark for immersed geometric compositions.

Remark 3.2

(Notation and Splittings for the Lagrangian Immersion)
.

We denote by
\widetilde{L}_{02}:=M_{02}^{-}\times\Delta_{1}\cap L_{0211}\subset M_{0211}=M_{0}\times M_{2}^{-}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{1}
the fibre product
L_{01}\times_{M_{1}}L_{12}\subset M_{0}^{-}\times M_{1}\times M_{1}^{-}\times M_{2}
after permuting components.
Then the Lagrangian immersion is given by projection to the first factors in
M_{0211}=M_{02}^{-}\times M_{11}
,

{\rm Pr}_{L_{02}}\,:\;\widetilde{L}_{02}\;\to\;L_{02}\,:=\;L_{01}\circ L_{12}\;\subset\;M_{02}.

Next, note that
\hat{u}:\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{L}_{02}
is a lift of
{\rm Pr}_{L_{02}}\circ\hat{u}=u_{02}|_{t=0}:\mathbb{R}\to L_{02}
. So if
{\rm Pr}_{L_{02}}
is an embedding, then the linearizations
{\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}:={\rm T}{\rm Pr}_{L_{02}}:{\rm T}\widetilde{L}_{02}\to{\rm T}L_{02}
induce isomorphisms

(15)

{\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}\,:\;{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\widetilde{L}_{02}\;\overset{\sim}{\to}\;{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02}.

When
{\rm Pr}_{L_{02}}
is an immersion, then
{\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}({\rm T}_{\hat{u}(s)}\widetilde{L}_{02})\subset{\rm T}_{u_{02}(s,0)}M_{02}
are still Lagrangian subspaces for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
(see
[
WW10
, Lemma 2.0.5]
), so that it makes sense to define the immersed tangent spaces

{\rm T}_{u_{02}(s,0)}L_{02}:={\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}({\rm T}_{\hat{u}(s)}\widetilde{L}_{02})
– which makes (
15
) an isomorphism by definition.
This allows us to introduce an orthogonal splitting for the immersed Lagrangian tangent spaces

(\pi_{02}\times\pi_{02}^{\perp})\,:\;{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}M_{02}\;\overset{\sim}{\to}\;{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02}\times({\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02})^{\perp}.

Now another consequence of immersed composition
[
WW12
, (18)]
is the direct sum

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}

\displaystyle\;=\;C_{\hat{u}}\;\oplus\;{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\widetilde{L}_{02}\;\oplus\;({\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02})^{\perp}\times\{0\}\;\oplus\;\{0\}\times{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle\text{with }\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}L_{0211}

\displaystyle\;=\;C_{\hat{u}}\;\oplus\;{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\widetilde{L}_{02},

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}(M_{02}\times\Delta_{1})

\displaystyle\;=\;{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\widetilde{L}_{02}\;\oplus\;({\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02})^{\perp}\times\{0\}\;\oplus\;\{0\}\times{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1}.

This yields an explicit projection
\pi_{02}^{\perp}\times\pi_{11}:{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}\to({\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02})^{\perp}\times{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1}
to a complement of
{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}(M_{02}\times\Delta_{1})\cap{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}L_{0211}={\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\widetilde{L}_{02}\subset{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}(M_{02}\times\Delta_{1})
.
Here we fix a global projection to the diagonal such as

\pi_{11}:{\rm T}M_{11}\to({\rm T}\Delta_{1})^{\perp},(\xi_{1},\xi^{\prime}_{1})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi^{\prime}_{1},\xi_{1}+\xi^{\prime}_{1})
with
M_{11}:=M_{1}^{-}\times M_{1}
.

Based on Remark
3.1
we will show Fredholm properties for
\mathcal{F}_{0}
in Lemma
3.3
– after completion with respect to the norms

\displaystyle\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}

\displaystyle\,:=\;\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{3/2}}+\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\nabla_{t}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))},

\displaystyle\|(\eta_{02},\hat{\eta})\|^{\Omega}_{0}

\displaystyle\,:=\;\|\eta_{02}\|_{H^{1/2}}+\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}.

These norms are chosen to complete
\Gamma
and
\Omega
to the Banach spaces

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{0}

\displaystyle\;\subset\;H^{3/2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\;\times\;\bigl(W^{1,1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))\cap W^{2,1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))\bigr),

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}_{0}

\displaystyle\;=\;H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\;\times\;\bigl(L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))\cap W^{1,1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))\bigr),

where
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}
is the closed subspace specified by the Lagrangian boundary conditions and slice condition.
Now we need to understand the kernel and cokernel of the linearization
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)
at
\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)=0

to prove its Fredholm property and – towards completing the data of an adiabatic Fredholm family – construct an appropriate projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{K}:=\ker{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)
and representation
\operatorname{coker}{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\simeq\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
by a subspace of smooth sections.

Lemma 3.3
.

The continuous extension of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}:\overline{\Gamma}_{0}\to\overline{\Omega}_{0}
to the completions of

(\Gamma,\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{0})
and
(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{0})
is a Fredholm operator with
kernel

\displaystyle\ker\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}

\displaystyle\;=\;\ker{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\;\simeq\;\ker{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}

\displaystyle\;=\;\bigl\{(\mathfrak{k}_{02},\hat{\mathfrak{k}})\,\big|\,\mathfrak{k}_{02}\in\ker{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag},\hat{\mathfrak{k}}={\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}^{-1}\mathfrak{k}_{02}|_{t=0}\bigr\}\;=:\,\mathfrak{K}\;\subset\;\Gamma

and cokernel
\operatorname{coker}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\simeq\operatorname{coker}\ker{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}\simeq(\operatorname{im}{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag})^{\perp_{L^{2}}}

represented by

\mathfrak{C}\,:=\;\bigl\{(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\,\big|\,\mathfrak{c}_{02}\in(\operatorname{im}{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag})^{\perp_{L^{2}}}\bigr\}\;\subset\;\Omega.

Here
{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}
is the linearized operator of the original
\epsilon=0
PDE – the Cauchy-Riemann equation for strips in
M_{02}
with Lagrangian boundary conditions,

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}\,:\;\bigl\{\xi_{02}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})\,\big|\,\xi_{02}|_{t=0}\in{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=0}}L_{02},\xi_{02}|_{t=1}\in{\rm T}_{u_{02}|_{t=1}}(L_{0}\times L_{2})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\;\;\to\;\hskip 20.00003ptL^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1],{\rm T}_{u_{02}}M_{02})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\xi_{02}\hskip 20.00003pt\;\mapsto\;\nabla_{t}\xi_{02}-J_{02}(u_{02})\nabla_{s}\xi_{02}-\nabla_{\xi_{02}}J_{02}\partial_{s}u_{02}.

In particular, this identifies the Fredholm index of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
with the index of
{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}
.
Moreover, for any projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}_{02}}:H^{1}(\ldots)\to\mathfrak{K}_{02}:=\ker{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}
and choice of norm on
\mathfrak{K}_{02}
we obtain a stabilized Fredholm estimate with some constant
C_{0}

for all
(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\overline{\Gamma}_{0},(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\in\mathfrak{C}
,

\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}+\|(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq C_{0}\bigl(\|\pi_{\mathfrak{K}_{02}}(\xi_{02})\|^{\mathfrak{K}_{02}}+\|\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})-(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\|^{\Omega}_{0}\bigr).

The proof is deferred to the end of this section.
Comparing with the abstract formulation in Definition
2.1
, the corresponding projection to the kernel

\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{K}
can then be obtained by
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi}):=\bigl(\mathfrak{k}_{02}=\pi_{\mathfrak{K}_{02}}(\xi_{02}),{\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}^{-1}\mathfrak{k}_{02}|_{t=0}\bigr)
. These particular constructions of projection to the kernel and cokernel representation only interacting with the first component in
\Gamma
are crucial to obtain the
\epsilon
-dependent properties of an adiabatic Fredholm family in the following announcement of a theorem – whose full proof is in the process of being written up in the general context of multi-strip-shrinking in any quilt.

Theorem 3.4
.

Given a choice of projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}:\Gamma\to\mathfrak{K}
and cokernel representation
\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega

as in Lemma
3.3
, there exist constants
C_{0},C_{1},C_{\mathfrak{C}}\in(0,\infty)
and continuous functions

c:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
,
c_{\Delta}:\Delta\to[0,\infty)

that supplement the above data
14
14

14

As defined, the norm bounds
\|\cdot\|^{\bullet}_{0}\leq\|\cdot\|^{\bullet}_{\epsilon}
will hold up to a uniform constant. Alternatively, we can meet these bounds precisely by multipling the norms
\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{0}
with fixed constants.

\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in[0,1]},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\bigr)

to form a regularizing
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-regular adiabatic Fredholm family as in Definition
2.1
,
2.13
.

Sketch of Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

We begin by going through the conditions Definition
2.1
.

Openness of Domain:

\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\,:=\;\bigl\{(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\Gamma\,\big|\,\|\xi_{02}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}},\|\hat{\xi}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}<\text{injectivity radius}\bigr\}
is open in the relative topology of
\Gamma\subset\overline{\Gamma}_{0}

by the Sobolev embedding
H^{3/2}\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}
on domains of dimension
2
and the Sobolev embeddings
W^{1,1}\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}
and
H^{1}\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}
on domains of dimension
1
.

Lower Bound on
\Gamma
Norms:

\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\leq\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}

holds up to a constant for all
(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\Gamma
and
\epsilon\in[0,1]
by the Sobolev embedding

H^{2}\subset H^{3/2}
on domains of dimension
2
in the first component, and in the second component by the characterization

H^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])\;=\;H^{2}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap H^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))\cap L^{2}([0,1],H^{2}(\mathbb{R}))

and the inclusions
H^{2}=W^{2,2}\subset W^{2,1}
and
H^{1}=W^{1,2}\subset W^{1,1}
on compact domains. However, to obtain an
\epsilon
-independent constant, note that for
0<\epsilon\leq 1
we can use
\epsilon^{-1}\geq 1
to obtain

\displaystyle\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{3/2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\nabla_{t}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle\geq\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{3/2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\nabla_{t}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle=\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}-\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))},

so to obtain a uniform constant in
\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\leq C\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}

(which can then be used to rescale the
\epsilon=0
norm to make the bound hold without a constant)
it remains to bound

\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}
by
\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{3/2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}
.
This follows from the boundary condition
\hat{\xi}_{02}|_{t=0}=\xi_{02}|_{t=0}
and the bounded inverse of the isomorphism in Remark
3.1
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\pi_{02}\,\hat{\xi}_{02}|_{t=0}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\pi_{02}\,\xi_{02}|_{t=0}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\bigl(\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{3/2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])}\bigr).

Lower Bound on
\Omega
Norms:

For
\epsilon\in(0,1]
we can use
\epsilon^{-1}\geq 1
to obtain

\displaystyle\|(\eta_{02},\hat{\eta})\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\|\eta_{02}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\eta_{02}\|_{H^{1}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\hat{\eta}\|_{H^{1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle\geq\|\eta_{02}\|_{H^{1/2}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\;\geq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\|(\eta_{02},\hat{\eta})\|^{\Omega}_{0}.

So
\|(\eta_{02},\hat{\eta})\|^{\Omega}_{0}\leq\|(\eta_{02},\hat{\eta})\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

holds up to a uniform constant which can then be used to rescale the
\epsilon=0
norm to meet the exact requirement.

Fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Regularity:

The differentials of
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
are uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{0}
by the Uniform Continuity of
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
proven below – so in fact this uniform continuity in
\Gamma
is uniform in
\epsilon\in[0,1]
.
So it remains to check that
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{0}
for a fixed
\epsilon\in[0,1]
– which follows (even uniformly in
\epsilon
) from the Uniform Bounds on
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
proven below.

Fredholm:

The continuous extensions of the linearization
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)}:\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
are Fredholm operators for
\epsilon=0
by Lemma
3.3
and for
\epsilon>0
by
[
WW12
]

Index:

[
WW12
, Lemma 2.1.3]
identifies the Fredholm index of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{1}(0)}
with that of
{\rm D}_{u_{02}}^{\rm Lag}
, which Lemma
3.3
identifies with the index of
\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
. Moreover, the Fredholm indices for
0<\epsilon\leq 1
are constant since this is a continuous family of Fredholm operators between fixed Banach spaces.

Furthermore, Lemma
3.3
shows that the kernel
\mathfrak{K}=\ker\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}
is contained in the dense subset of smooth sections
\Gamma
, and that the cokernel
\operatorname{coker}\overline{{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)}\simeq\mathfrak{C}
can be represented by a subset
\mathfrak{C}\subset\Omega
of the smooth sections.

\mathbf{\epsilon=0}
Fredholm Estimate:

This is proven in Lemma
3.3
.

Uniform Fredholm-ish Estimate:

[
WW12
, Lemma 3.2.1]
shows that for all
(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\Gamma
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
we have

c_{1}\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\|\xi_{02}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{1/2}\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\hat{\xi}|_{t=1}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\xi_{02}|_{t=1}\|_{H^{1}}.

Then we bound
\|\xi_{02}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\xi_{02}|_{t=1}\|_{H^{1}}\leq\|\xi_{02}\|_{H^{3/2}}\leq\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
(up to a uniform constant) by the Sobolev trace theorem. For the second component the estimates for
W^{1,1}([0,1])\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,1])\subset L^{2}([0,1])
give (up to a uniform constant)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{1/2}\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\hat{\xi}|_{t=1}\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{\xi}\|_{W^{1,1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\hat{\xi}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{\xi}\|_{W^{1,1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle=\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}\;\leq\;\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}.

So this combines to a uniform constant
C_{1}
in

\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{1}\bigl(\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{0}\bigr)
.

Uniform Cokernel Bound:

With the specific choice of cokernel representation from Lemma
3.3
we have for
\epsilon\in(0,1]

and for all
\mathfrak{c}=(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\in\mathfrak{C}

\|(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\;=\;\|\mathfrak{c}_{02}\|_{H^{1}}\;\leq\;C_{\mathfrak{C}}\|\mathfrak{c}_{02}\|_{H^{1/2}}\;=\;C_{\mathfrak{C}}\|(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\|^{\Omega}_{0}

with a uniform constant
C_{\mathfrak{C}}
since all norms on a finite dimensional space such as
\mathfrak{C}_{02}
are equivalent.

Quadratic-ish Estimate:

[
WW12
, Lemma 3.1.5]
proves

\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{02},\hat{\gamma})(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0,0)(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\leq c(\|(\gamma_{02},\hat{\gamma})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon})\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}

for all
(\gamma_{02},\hat{\gamma})\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
,
(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\Gamma
, and
\epsilon\in[0,1]
with a linear function
c(x)=C_{2}x
.

Continuity of Derivatives at
\mathbf{0}
:

For
\xi=(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\Gamma
and
0<\epsilon\leq 1
we have – since the first component is independent of
\epsilon
–

\displaystyle\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\xi-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)\xi\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{0}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\bigl(\;0\,,\,\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}-\epsilon\,\hat{J}(\hat{u})\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}-\epsilon\,\nabla_{\hat{\xi}}\hat{J}\,\partial_{s}\hat{u}\;-\;\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\;\bigr)\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{0}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\,\bigl(\bigl\|\hat{J}(\hat{u})\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}-\epsilon\,\nabla_{\hat{\xi}}\hat{J}\,\partial_{s}\hat{u}\bigr\|_{L^{1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))}\;+\;\bigl\|\nabla_{t}\bigl(\hat{J}(\hat{u})\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}-\epsilon\,\nabla_{\hat{\xi}}\hat{J}\,\partial_{s}\hat{u}\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{1}([0,1],L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\,C_{u}\bigl(\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{t}\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\,C_{u}\epsilon^{-1/2}\bigl(\epsilon^{1/2}\|\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{1/2}\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{1/2}\|\nabla_{s}^{2}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{-1/2}\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{-1/2}\|\nabla_{t}\nabla_{s}\hat{\xi}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq c_{\Delta}(\epsilon)\|(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}

with
c_{\Delta}(x)=x^{1/2}\,C_{u}
with a constant
C_{u}
that is determined by
\hat{J}
and
\hat{u}
.

Near-Solution:

The family was constructed near a solution
(u_{02},\hat{u})
of the
\epsilon=0
problem, which corresponds to
\mathcal{F}_{0}(0)=0
, and
[
WW12
, Lemma 3.1.5]
proves

\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{1}\epsilon^{1/4}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Then it remains to spell out and verify the conditions of Definition
2.13
.

Regularizing:

The meaning of this property is, first, that solutions of a nonlinear equation with smooth right hand side are smooth

(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}.

Then the second requirement is that solutions of a linearized equation – at a smooth base point – with smooth right hand side are smooth

(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty},\hskip 10.00002pt(\xi^{\prime}_{02},\hat{\xi}^{\prime})\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})(\xi^{\prime}_{02},\hat{\xi}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt(\xi^{\prime}_{02},\hat{\xi}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}.

For
\epsilon>0
both statements follow from standard elliptic regularity.
For
\epsilon=0
the elliptic regularity for the Cauchy-Riemann operators on
M_{02}
needs to be combined with the regularity that follows from the isomorphisms of Remark
3.1
for arbitrarily large
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
s\geq 0
:
Suppose we have already established
\xi_{02}\in H^{s}
for some
s\geq 3/2
.
Then
\hat{\xi}\in W^{1,1}([0,1],H^{1}(\mathbb{R},{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}\hat{M}))
satisfies
\nabla_{t}\hat{\xi}=\hat{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
and the boundary conditions

\hat{\xi}_{11}|_{t=0}\in{\rm T}_{\hat{u}_{11}}\Delta_{1}
,
\hat{\xi}|_{t=1}\in{\rm T}_{\hat{u}}L_{0211}
,
and
\pi_{02}\,\hat{\xi}_{02}|_{t=0}=\pi_{02}\,\xi_{02}|_{t=0}\in H^{s-1/2}(\mathbb{R})
, and thus

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}(\cdot,t)={\rm Pr}_{{\rm T}L_{02}}^{-1}\pi_{02}\,\xi_{02}|_{t=0}\;+\;\textstyle\int_{0}^{t}\hat{\eta}\;-\;(\pi_{02}^{\perp}\times\pi_{11})\bigl(\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\hat{\eta}\bigr)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,1],H^{s-1/2}(\mathbb{R})).

Pointwise Continuity in
\mathbf{\Delta}
at Solutions Modulo
\mathbf{\mathfrak{C}}
:

Given any
\epsilon_{0}\in[0,1]
and a solution
(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})=(\mathfrak{c}_{02},0)\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})-\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\bigl(\;0\,,\,\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\partial_{t}-\epsilon\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})-\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\partial_{t}-\epsilon_{0}\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\;\bigr)\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=|\epsilon-\epsilon_{0}|\bigl\|\bigl(\;0\,,\,\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\;\bigr)\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}.

To show that this converges to
0
as
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}
we need to show that the
\epsilon
-norm of
\hat{\eta}:=\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})
doesn’t blow up faster than
|\epsilon-\epsilon_{0}|^{-1}
.
For
\epsilon>0
this norm is

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1/2}\bigl(\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)+\epsilon^{-3/2}\|\nabla_{t}\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{-3/4}\|\hat{\eta}\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt=\epsilon^{-1/2}\bigl(\|\partial_{s}\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\|_{L^{2}}+\bigl\|\nabla_{s}\bigl(\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\epsilon^{-3/2}\bigl\|\nabla_{t}\bigl(\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{-3/4}\|\partial_{s}\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\|_{L^{4}}.

This is bounded in case
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}>0
.
However, for
\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}=0
we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl\|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})-\mathcal{F}_{0}(\xi_{02},\hat{\xi})\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\;=\;\epsilon^{1/2}\bigl(\|\partial_{s}\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{s}\bigl(\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\epsilon^{-1/2}\|\nabla_{t}\bigl(\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)\|_{L^{2}}+\epsilon^{1/4}\|\partial_{s}\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})\|_{L^{4}},

which will converge to
0
only if
\nabla_{t}\bigl(\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi})^{-1}(\hat{J}\partial_{s})\,\hat{e}(\hat{\xi})=0
, that is if we can ensure that all components are independent of
t\in[0,1]
. This is true for
\hat{u}
since it arises from an
\epsilon_{0}=0
solution. It holds for parallel transport
\Phi
, exponential map
\hat{e}
