\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{c},\gamma)-Q(0)(\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\mathcal{F}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})

\displaystyle=Q(0)\bigl(P(0)(\mathfrak{c},\gamma)-(\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\mathcal{F}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})\bigr)

\displaystyle=Q(0)\bigl((\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma),{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma-\mathfrak{c})-(\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\mathcal{F}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})\bigr)\;=\;Q(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma-\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\bigr).

Now given any
w=(\mathfrak{c},\gamma),w^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}_{W}=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
, we estimate

\displaystyle\bigl\|B(\mathfrak{k},w^{\prime})-B(\mathfrak{k},w)\bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|Q(0)(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma^{\prime}-\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}))-Q(0)(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma-\mathcal{F}(\gamma))\bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|Q(0)\bigl(0,{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma^{\prime}-\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma+\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\bigr)\bigr\|^{W}

using (
25
) in Lemma
4.4

\displaystyle\leq C_{Q}\bigl(\|0\|^{\mathfrak{K}}+\bigl\|\mathcal{F}(\gamma)-\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\bigr\|^{\Omega}\bigr)

using the convexity of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
to ensure
\gamma^{\prime}+\lambda(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
for
0\leq\lambda\leq 1

\displaystyle=C_{Q}\bigl\|\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl({\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma+\lambda(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}))(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\bigr){\rm d}\lambda\bigr\|^{\Omega}

\displaystyle\leq C_{Q}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(\gamma^{\prime}+\lambda(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}))(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})-{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\bigr)\bigr\|^{\Omega}{\rm d}\lambda

using (
37
)

\displaystyle\leq C_{Q}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}c\bigl(\gamma^{\prime}+\lambda(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}),0\bigr){\rm d}\lambda\;\|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\|^{\Gamma},

where
c:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is the monotone continuous function with
c(0)=0
from (
37
).
So, given any
\theta\in(0,1)
we can find
\delta_{\theta}>0
so that
c(\delta)\leq\frac{1}{C_{Q}}\theta
for all
0\leq\delta\leq\delta_{\theta}
. Then
w=(\mathfrak{c},\gamma),w^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\in\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{W}|\|v\|^{W}\leq\delta_{\theta}\}
implies
\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma},\|\gamma^{\prime}\|^{\Gamma}\leq\delta_{\theta}
, which guarantees
\|\gamma^{\prime}+\lambda(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\|^{\Gamma}\leq\lambda\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}+(1-\lambda)\|\gamma^{\prime}\|^{\Gamma}\leq\delta_{\theta}
for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
, and hence the desired contraction property (
38
)

\displaystyle\bigl\|B(\mathfrak{k},w^{\prime})-B(\mathfrak{k},w)\bigr\|^{W}

\displaystyle\leq C_{Q}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{C_{Q}}\theta{\rm d}\lambda\|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\|^{\Gamma}=\theta\|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\|^{\Gamma}.

To establish smallness on
B(\mathfrak{k},0)
we again use (
25
) and the assumption
\mathcal{F}(0)=0
to obtain

\bigl\|B(\mathfrak{k},0)\bigr\|^{W}\;=\;\bigl\|\overline{Q}(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)0-\mathcal{F}(0)\bigr)\bigr\|^{W}\;\leq\;C_{Q}\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{\mathfrak{K}}\;<\;\delta

for all
\mathfrak{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K},\delta}:=\{\mathfrak{k}\in\mathfrak{K}\,|\,\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{\mathfrak{K}}<\frac{\delta}{C_{Q}}\}
, as claimed in (
39
).
Finally, if the Fredholm map is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular, then the maps
B:(\mathfrak{k},\gamma)\mapsto Q(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)\gamma-\mathcal{F}(\gamma)\bigr)
are
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
as well. Indeed,
\overline{Q}(0)
and
{\rm D}\mathcal{F}(0)
are linear operators, so that
B
inherits its regularity from
\mathcal{F}
.
∎

To generalize this contraction formulation to an adiabatic Fredholm family, we first formulate the simpler version when the family is regularizing, then provide the general contraction formulation.

Lemma 4.7
.

Given a regularizing adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
as in Definitions
2.1
and
2.8
, let
Q_{\epsilon}(0)=\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)|_{\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega}:\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega\to\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
be the bijections from Lemma
4.5
for
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}\subset\Delta
.
Then the union of zero sets
\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\subset\Delta_{Q}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
is naturally identified with the zero set of the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{Q}\,:\;\Delta_{Q}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma,

\displaystyle(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\mathfrak{c},Q_{\epsilon}(0)(\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})\bigr),

Moreover, each of the maps
\mathcal{G}_{Q}(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},\cdot,\cdot)
is close to the identity map on
\mathcal{V}_{W}:=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset W:=\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
up to finite dimensional factors and a contraction in the following sense:
We can write

\mathcal{G}_{Q}\,:\;\Delta_{Q}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{W}\;\rightarrow\;\mathfrak{C}\times W,\hskip 20.00003pt(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},w)\;\mapsto\;\bigl(A_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w),w-B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)\bigr),

where
A_{\epsilon}:\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{W}\to\mathfrak{C}
maps to the finite dimensional space
\mathfrak{C}
by projection
A_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=\mathfrak{c}
, and

(40)

B_{\epsilon}:\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{W}\to W,\hskip 10.00002ptB_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=Q_{\epsilon}(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma-\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\bigr)

is a family of contractions near
0\in W
parametrized by
(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\in\Delta_{Q}\times\mathfrak{K}
.
More precisely, if we equip
W
with the norms
\|w=(\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}:=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
, then given any
\theta\in(0,1)
there exists
\delta_{\theta}>0
such that for all
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q},\mathfrak{k}\in\mathfrak{K},w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{W}
we have

\displaystyle\|w\|^{W}_{\epsilon},\|w^{\prime}\|^{W}_{\epsilon}\leq\delta_{\theta}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w^{\prime})-B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}\leq\theta\|w^{\prime}-w\|^{W}_{\epsilon}.

In addition, given any
\delta>0
we can find open neighbourhoods
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K},\delta}\subset\mathfrak{K}
of
0
and
\Delta_{\delta}\subset\Delta_{Q}
of
0
with

\displaystyle(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\in\Delta_{\delta}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K},\delta}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},0)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}<\delta.

Lemma 4.8
.

Given an adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
as in Definitions
2.1
,
let
\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0):\mathfrak{K}\times\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
be the isomorphisms from Lemma
4.5
for
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}\subset\Delta
.
Then the union of completed zero sets
\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\simeq\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}^{-1}(0_{\Delta_{Q}})
is naturally identified with the preimage of the "zero section"
0_{\Delta_{Q}}:=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\{(0,0,0)\}\subset\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}
under the fibrewise continuous extension of
\mathcal{G}_{Q}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}\,:\;\textstyle\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\textstyle\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon},

\displaystyle(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(\epsilon,\mathfrak{c},\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)(\overline{\pi}_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})\bigr).

Moreover, each of the maps
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},\cdot,\cdot)
is – up to finite dimensional factors and a contraction – close to the identity map on the interior
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}:={\rm int}(\overline{\mathcal{V}_{W}})=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}
of the closure of
\mathcal{V}_{W}=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
inside the completed space
\overline{W}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{W}^{\|\cdot\|^{W}_{\epsilon}}=\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
in the following sense: We can write for
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\,:\;\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\;\rightarrow\;\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{W}_{\epsilon},\hskip 20.00003pt(\mathfrak{k},w)\;\mapsto\;\bigl(\overline{A}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w),w-\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)\bigr),

where
\overline{A}_{\epsilon}:\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{C}
maps to the finite dimensional space
\mathfrak{C}
by projection
\overline{A}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=\mathfrak{c}
,
and
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}:\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\to\overline{W}_{\epsilon}
given by
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma-\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\bigr)
is a family of contractions near
0\in\overline{W}_{\epsilon}
parametrized by
(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\in\Delta_{Q}\times\mathfrak{K}
.
More precisely, if we equip
\overline{W}_{\epsilon}
with the norm
\|w=(\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}:=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\Omega}_{0}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
, then given any
\theta\in(0,1)
there exists
\delta_{\theta}>0
such that for all
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q},\mathfrak{k}\in\mathfrak{K},w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{W}
we have

(41)

\displaystyle\|w\|^{W}_{\epsilon},\|w^{\prime}\|^{W}_{\epsilon}\leq\delta_{\theta}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w^{\prime})-\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}\leq\theta\|w^{\prime}-w\|^{W}_{\epsilon}.

In addition, given any
\delta>0
we can find open neighbourhoods
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K},\delta}\subset\mathfrak{K}
of
0
and
\Delta_{\delta}\subset\Delta_{Q}
of
0
with

(42)

\displaystyle(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\in\Delta_{\delta}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K},\delta}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},0)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}<\delta.

If the adiabatic Fredholm family is fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular as in Definition
2.10
for some
\ell\geq 1
, then each contraction
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}:\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\to\overline{W}_{\epsilon}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
as well.

Finally, if the adiabatic Fredholm family is regularizing, then the maps
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot)
,
\overline{A}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}
are the continuous extensions of the maps
\mathcal{G}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot)
,
A_{\epsilon}
and
B_{\epsilon}
in Lemma
4.7
w.r.t. the norms
\|\cdot\|^{W}_{\epsilon}
on
W
,
\|\cdot\|^{\mathfrak{K}}=\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{K}
, and
\|\cdot\|^{\mathfrak{C}}=\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{0}
on
\mathfrak{C}
.

Proof of Lemmas
4.7
and
4.8
.

From Lemma
4.3
we have
\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\simeq\mathcal{G}^{-1}(0)
for
\mathcal{G}:\Delta\times\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega
given by
\mathcal{G}(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=(\mathfrak{c},\pi_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})=(\mathfrak{c},\overline{g}_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{c},\gamma))
.
The map considered here is obtained by composing
\mathcal{G}
with
{\rm Id}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times Q_{\epsilon}(0):\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega\to\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
. Here
Q_{\epsilon}(0):\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega\to\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
is a bijection by Lemma
4.5
, so this composition does not affect the zero set.

When we drop the regularizing condition, then Lemma
4.5
only asserts bijectivity of the linearized operators
{\rm D}\overline{g}_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{k}}(\mathfrak{c}_{0},\gamma_{0})
after taking
\epsilon
-dependent completions, that is we need to work with the inverse operators
\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0):\mathfrak{K}\times\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
.
These can be composed with the "section"
\overline{\mathcal{G}}:\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\rightarrow\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
from Lemma
4.3
to obtain the claimed identification

\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}}\{\epsilon\}\times\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(0)\simeq\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(0_{\Delta_{Q}})\simeq\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}^{-1}(0_{\Delta_{Q}}).

Here
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}=({\rm Id}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0))\circ\overline{\mathcal{G}}:\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\rightarrow\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\Delta}\{\epsilon\}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
is given by

\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=\bigl(\epsilon,\mathfrak{c},\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)(\overline{\pi}_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{k},\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)-\mathfrak{c})\bigr)=\bigl(\epsilon,\overline{A}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma),(\mathfrak{c},\gamma)-\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\bigr),

where

\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma-\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\bigr)
follows as in Lemma
4.6
.
When the family is regularizing, then Lemma
4.5
ensures that the inverse
\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)
restricts to
Q_{\epsilon}(0)=\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)|_{\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega}:\mathfrak{K}\times\Omega\to\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
, and thus
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot)
,
\overline{A}_{\epsilon}
, resp.
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}
restrict to the claimed expressions for
\mathcal{G}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot)
,
A_{\epsilon}
and
B_{\epsilon}
.

Next, we will establish continuity of
\overline{A}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}
, and hence of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot)=(\overline{A}_{\epsilon},{\rm Id}_{\overline{W}_{\epsilon}}-\overline{B}_{\epsilon})
, which in particular implies that these are in fact the continuous extensions of
A_{\epsilon}
,
B_{\epsilon}
, and
\mathcal{G}_{Q}(\epsilon,\cdot)
.
Continuity of the linear map
\overline{A}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)=\mathfrak{c}
is evident since
\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}\leq\|(\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\|^{W}_{\epsilon}=\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{0}
.
To establish continuity of
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}
for fixed
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}
we use boundedness of the operators

\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)
in Lemma
4.5
and the [Fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Regularity] of
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}
to deduce that for
(\mathfrak{k}_{i},\mathfrak{c}_{i},\gamma_{i})\to(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\in\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}_{i},\mathfrak{c}_{i},\gamma_{i})-\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\bigr\|^{W}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k}_{i}-\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)(\gamma_{i}-\gamma)-\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)+\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{i})\bigr)\bigr\|^{W}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq C_{Q}\bigl(\|\mathfrak{k}_{i}-\mathfrak{k}\|^{\mathfrak{K}}+\bigl\|{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)(\gamma_{i}-\gamma)\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}+\bigl\|\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)-\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma_{i})\bigr\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon}\bigr)\;\to\;0.

It remains to check the estimates for
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}
which then imply the same estimates for its restriction
B_{\epsilon}
in the regularizing case.
This is achieved by applying Lemma
4.6
to the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
Fredholm map
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma,\epsilon}\to\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}
and noting that the
[Quadratic-ish Estimate] (
37
) is provided by Lemma
2.2
with the
\epsilon
-independent function
c:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
that is a part of the data of the adiabatic Fredholm family.

Finally, the identification of the completion
\overline{W}_{\epsilon}:=\overline{W}^{\|\cdot\|^{W}_{\epsilon}}=\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
results from the fact that
\mathfrak{C}
is finite dimensional, so complete w.r.t. any norm, and
\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}=\overline{\Gamma}^{\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}}
is the completion w.r.t. the norm on the infinite dimensional factor of
W=\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
.
Similarly, the closure of
\mathcal{V}_{W}=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
inside the completed space
\overline{W}_{\epsilon}=\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
is the product of closures
\overline{\mathcal{V}_{W}}=\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}}
, and then the interior is the product of interiors
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}={\rm int}(\overline{\mathcal{V}_{W}})={\rm int}(\mathfrak{C}\times\overline{\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}})=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}
.

If the adiabatic Fredholm family is fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular, that is
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:(\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma},{\|\cdot\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}})\to(\Omega,\|\cdot\|^{\Omega}_{\epsilon})
is uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
for each
\epsilon\in\Delta
, then the maps
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}:(\mathfrak{k},\gamma)\mapsto\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)\bigl(\mathfrak{k},{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)\gamma-\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)\bigr)
are uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
as well for each fixed
\epsilon\in\Delta_{Q}
. Indeed,
\overline{Q}_{\epsilon}(0)
and
{\rm D}\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(0)
are linear operators and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}
inherits its regularity from
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
by Lemma
2.4
.
∎

4.3.
Solution Maps

This section constructs solution maps
\sigma_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to W=\mathfrak{C}\times\Gamma
that solve the fixed point equations
w=B_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)
from §
4.2
.
We also show how various regularity assumptions on the adiabatic Fredholm family
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
transfer to the contractions
B_{\epsilon}
and then the solution maps
\sigma_{\epsilon}
.
The latter is the technically hardest – and longest – part of the proof.
It can be understood as analogous to the properties of solution germs in
[
HWZ21
, Thm.3.3.3]
, which arguably are the technical core that allows polyfold regularization theory to construct transverse perturbations over spaces of maps modulo reparametrization.
Thus this section finishes the argument that adiabatic Fredholm theory is compatible with polyfold regularization theory.

Starting again in the classical Fredholm setting of Theorem
4.1
, we work here just with the second component of the map
\mathcal{G}_{Q}
that was constructed in Lemma
4.6
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Consider a Banach space
(\overline{W},\|\cdot\|)
, an open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\subset\overline{W}

containing
0=0_{\overline{W}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
, a set
\mathcal{V}_{K}
, and a map of the form

\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\;\rightarrow\;\overline{W},\hskip 20.00003pt(k,w)\;\mapsto\;w-B(k,w),

where

B:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\to\overline{W}
is a contraction near
0\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
parametrized by
k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
.
More precisely, assume that there exists
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\delta>0
such that for all
k\in\mathcal{V}_{K},w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
we have

(43)

\displaystyle\|w\|,\|w^{\prime}\|\leq\delta\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|B(k,w^{\prime})-B(k,w)\|\leq\theta\|w^{\prime}-w\|,

and moreover
\{w\in\overline{W}\,|\,\|w\|^{\overline{W}}\leq\delta\}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
as well as

(44)

\displaystyle\forall k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\hskip 10.00002pt\|B(k,0)\|<(1-\theta)\delta.

Then there is a unique solution map
\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
that parametrizes the zero set of the map (i.e. the fixed points of
B
) near
\mathcal{V}_{K}\times 0\subset\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
, that is

\bigl\{(k,w)\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\,\big|\,w-B(k,w)=0,\|w\|<\delta\bigr\}=\bigl\{(k,\sigma(k))\,\big|\,k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\bigr\}.

In case
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}=\overline{W}
we can drop the assumption (
44
) as long as (
43
) holds for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\overline{W}
. In that case the conclusion is

\bigl\{(k,w)\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\overline{W}\,\big|\,w-B(k,w)=0\bigr\}=\bigl\{(k,\sigma(k))\,\big|\,k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\bigr\}
.

If, moreover,
\mathcal{V}_{K}
is equipped with a topology such that
B:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\to\overline{W}
is continuous, then the solution map
\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
is continuous.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{V}_{K}\subset K
is an open subset of a normed vector space
so that
B:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\to\overline{W}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
, then the solution map
\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
with
{\rm D}\sigma(k)=\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k,\sigma(k))\bigr)^{-1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k,\sigma(k))
.

Proof.

To apply the Banach Fixed Point Theorem to one of the contractions
B(k,\cdot)
, we need to find a closed subset of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
that is preserved by this map. This role will be played by the closed ball
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}:=\{w\in\overline{W}\,\|\,\|w\|^{\overline{W}}\leq\delta\}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
of radius
\delta
centered at
0
, which by assumption is contained in the domain where
B(k,\cdot)
is defined.
Moreover, this ball is invariant under
B(k,\cdot)
if we further restrict ourselves to
k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
. Indeed, we have
B(k,\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta})\subset\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}
since for all
w\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}

\|B(k,w)\|\leq\|B(k,w)-B(k,0)\|+\|B(k,0)\|\leq\theta\|w-0\|+(1-\theta)\delta\leq\theta\delta+(1-\theta)\delta=\delta.

Now the Banach Fixed Point Theorem applies to the contraction
B(k,\cdot):\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}\to\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}
for each
k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
to guarantee that each such map has a unique fixed point
w_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}
. This defines a map
\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta},k\mapsto w_{k}
, which by definition parametrizes the solutions: For
w\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}

we have

w-B(k,w)=0,\|w\|\leq\delta\hskip 10.00002pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptw=B(k,w),w\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}\hskip 10.00002pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptw=\sigma(k).

Here we can replace the condition
\|w\|\leq\delta
by
\|w\|<\delta

because the norm of the actual solutions is strictly less than
\delta
due to the strict inequality in (
44
),

\displaystyle\|\sigma(k)\|

\displaystyle\;=\;\|B(k,\sigma(k))\|\;\leq\;\|B(k,\sigma(k))-B(k,0)\|+\|B(k,0)\|

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\theta\|\sigma(k)-0\|+\|B(k,0)\|\;\leq\;\theta\delta+\|B(k,0)\|\;<\;\theta\delta+(1-\theta)\delta\;=\;\delta.

In case
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}=\overline{W}
the Banach Fixed Point Theorem applies for all
k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
with
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\overline{W},\delta}
replaced by
\overline{W}
to prove the same conclusion with no need for assumption (
44
) or a bound
\|w\|\leq\delta
.

If, moreover,
B:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\to\overline{W}
is continuous, then we use the defining equation
\sigma(k)=B(k,\sigma(k))
of the solution map and the contraction property (
43
) to estimate for
k_{0},k_{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{K}

\displaystyle\|\sigma(k_{1})-\sigma(k_{0})\|

\displaystyle=\|B(k_{1},\sigma(k_{1}))-B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\|B(k_{1},\sigma(k_{1}))-B(k_{1},\sigma(k_{0}))\|+\|B(k_{1},\sigma(k_{0}))-B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\theta\|\sigma(k_{1})-\sigma(k_{0})\|+\|B(k_{1},\sigma(k_{0}))-B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|

(45)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\|\sigma(k_{1})-\sigma(k_{0})\|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\|B(k_{1},\sigma(k_{0}))-B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|.

So continuity of
B
at
(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))
implies continuity of
\sigma
at
k_{0}
.

If, furthermore,
B:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}\to\overline{W}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, then we will show that the solution map
\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
as well. Towards that, we write the differential
{\rm D}B(k_{0},w_{0}):K\times\overline{W}\to\overline{W}
at
(k_{0},w_{0})
as the sum
{\rm D}B(k_{0},w_{0})(k,w)={\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},w_{0})k+{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0})w
of its partial differentials
{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},w_{0}):K\to\overline{W}
and
{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0}):\overline{W}\to\overline{W}
.
Each of these partial differentials is a bounded linear operator and varies continuously with the base point
(k_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
. Moreover,
{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(\overline{W},\overline{W})
inherits a linear contraction property from (
43
): For all
w\in\overline{W}
we have

\|{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0})w\|=\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-1}\|B(k_{0},w_{0}+hw)-B(k_{0},w_{0})\|\leq\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-1}\theta\|hw\|=\theta\|w\|.

This can also be stated as
\|{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0})\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{W},\overline{W})}\leq\theta
, and since
\theta<1
this implies that the operator
{\rm Id}_{\overline{W}}-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(\overline{W},\overline{W})
is invertible with
\|({\rm Id}_{\overline{W}}-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0}))^{-1}\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{W},\overline{W})}\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}
. Indeed, this holds for any
T\in\mathcal{L}(\overline{W},\overline{W})
with
\|T\|<1
, since
({\rm Id}_{\overline{W}}-T)^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}T^{n}
converges with norm bounded by
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\|T\|^{n}=\tfrac{1}{1-\|T\|}
.
Recall moreover that inverting these operators is a continuous map as in (
31
).
Thus we have established that the inverse operators
({\rm Id}_{\overline{W}}-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},w_{0}))^{-1}
exist and vary continuously with
(k_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
.
This is useful for proving differentiability of the solution map since symbolic differentiation of the defining equation
\sigma(k)=B(k,\sigma(k))
yields
{\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})k={\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))k+{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0})){\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})k
, and hence
\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr){\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})k={\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))k
. While this computation isn’t valid until differentiability of
\sigma
is established, we use it to recognize
\Phi(k_{0}):=\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)^{-1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\in\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W})
as the well defined candidate for the differential of
\sigma
at
(k_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
.
To establish that it is indeed the differential, we denote
\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k):=\sigma(k_{0}+k)-\sigma(k_{0})
and aim to show that its best linear approximation for small
\|k\|^{K}
is
\Phi(k_{0})k
.
For that purpose we first note that continuity of
{\rm D}B
at
(k_{0},w_{0}:=\sigma(k_{0}))
means that, given any
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

(46)

\|(k_{1},w_{1})-(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|^{K\times\overline{W}}<\delta_{\varepsilon}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl\|{\rm D}B(k_{1},w_{1})-{\rm D}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(K\times\overline{W},\overline{W})}<\varepsilon.

We can use this fact for
(k_{1},w_{1})=(k_{0}+k,\sigma(k_{0}))
to strengthen the continuity estimate (
45
) for
\sigma
by deducing that for
\|k\|^{K}<\delta_{\varepsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\|\;=\;\|\sigma(k_{0}+k)-\sigma(k)\|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\|B(k_{0}+k,\sigma(k_{0}))-B(k,\sigma(k_{0}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\bigl\|\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0}+\lambda k,\sigma(k_{0}))\,k\,{\rm d}\lambda\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\bigl(\|{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|^{\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W})}+\varepsilon\bigr)\|k\|^{K}.

Next, we use the defining equation
\sigma(k)=B(k,\sigma(k))
again to rewrite

\displaystyle\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)\bigl(\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)-\Phi(k_{0})k\bigr)

\displaystyle=B(k_{0}+k,\sigma(k_{0}+k))-B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigl(\sigma(k_{0}+k)-\sigma(k_{0})\bigr)-{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))k

\displaystyle=\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm D}B(k_{0}+\lambda k,\sigma(k_{0})+\lambda\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k))\bigl(k,\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\bigr){\rm d}\lambda-{\rm D}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigl(k,\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl({\rm D}B(k_{0}+\lambda k,\sigma(k_{0})+\lambda\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k))-{\rm D}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)\bigl(k,\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\bigr){\rm d}\lambda.

Thus for any
k_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
and sufficiently small
k\in K
– guaranteeing that
\{k_{0}+\lambda k\,|\,0\leq\lambda\leq 1\}\subset\mathcal{V}_{K}
– we can estimate

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sigma(k_{0}+k)-\sigma(k_{0})-\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)^{-1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))k\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)^{-1}\|^{\mathcal{L}(\overline{W},\overline{W})}\bigl\|\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)\bigl(\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)-\Phi(k_{0})k\bigr)\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|{\rm D}B(k_{0}+\lambda k,\sigma(k_{0})+\lambda\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k))-{\rm D}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(K\times\overline{W},\overline{W})}{\rm d}\lambda\bigl\|\bigl(k,\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\bigr)\bigr\|^{K\times\overline{W}}.

To proceed, we wish to use (
46
) at
(k_{1},w_{1})=(k_{\lambda},w_{\lambda}):=(k_{0}+\lambda k,\sigma(k_{0})+\lambda\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k))
. To check the assumption we use continuity of
\sigma
to note that given
\varepsilon>0
and the above
\delta_{\varepsilon}>0
there is
0<\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\{\delta_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\}
such that for all
k\in K
with
\|k\|^{K}<\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
we have
\|\sigma(k)-\sigma(k_{0})\|<\frac{1}{2}\min\{\delta_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\}
, and hence

\displaystyle\forall\;0\leq\lambda\leq 1:\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\|(k_{\lambda},w_{\lambda})-(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|^{K\times\overline{W}}

\displaystyle=\|(k_{0}+\lambda k,\sigma(k_{0})+\lambda\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k))-(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|^{K\times\overline{W}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\|k\|^{K}+\lambda\|\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\|\;<\;\min\{\delta_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\}.

Taking all this together, for every
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for
k\in K
with
\|k\|^{K}<\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sigma(k_{0}+k)-\sigma(k_{0})-\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)^{-1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))k\bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\|{\rm D}B(k_{\lambda},w_{\lambda})-{\rm D}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr\|^{\mathcal{L}(K\times\overline{W},\overline{W})}{\rm d}\lambda\bigl\|\bigl(k,\Delta\sigma(k_{0},k)\bigr)\bigr\|^{K\times\overline{W}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\,\varepsilon\,\bigl(1+\tfrac{1}{1-\theta}\bigl(\|{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\|^{\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W})}+\varepsilon\bigr)\bigr)\|k\|^{K},

which proves for
\|k\|^{K}\to 0
the convergence

\bigl\|\sigma(k_{0}+k)-\sigma(k_{0})-\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)^{-1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))k\bigr\|/\|k\|^{K}\;\longrightarrow\;0.

Thus
\sigma
is differentiable at all
k_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
with
{\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})=\bigl(\rm Id-{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))\bigr)^{-1}{\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))
. To verify that this differential varies continuously with
k_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
in
\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W})
, recall that
B
was assumed to be
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, thus
{\rm D}_{W}B
and
{\rm D}_{K}B
vary continuously in the operator norm with their base point in
\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
. Then continuity of
k_{0}\mapsto{\rm D}_{*}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))
for
*=K
and
*=W
follows from the continuity of
\sigma
. Finally, we already established above that the inverses
(\rm Id-T)^{-1}
exist and vary continuously with
T={\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))
. Thus
{\rm D}\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W})
is
\mathcal{C}^{0}
and
\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W}}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. This proves the Lemma for
\ell\leq 1
.

Towards proving the Lemma for
\ell\geq 2
note that the previous symbolic identity

{\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})={\rm D}_{K}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0}))+{\rm D}_{W}B(k_{0},\sigma(k_{0})){\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})
is now rigorous and can be understood to say that
\widetilde{\sigma}:\mathcal{V}_{K}\to\widetilde{W}:=\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W}),k_{0}\mapsto{\rm D}\sigma(k_{0})
is the solution map for the equation
\widetilde{w}=\widetilde{B}(k,\widetilde{w})
. Here

\widetilde{B}:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\widetilde{W}\to\widetilde{W}=\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W}),\hskip 10.00002pt(k,\widetilde{w})\mapsto{\rm D}_{K}B(k,\sigma(k))+{\rm D}_{W}B(k,\sigma(k))\circ\widetilde{w}

is a contraction that satisfies (
43
). Indeed, we have for all
k\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
and
\widetilde{w},\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}

\displaystyle\bigl\|\widetilde{B}(k,\widetilde{w}^{\prime})-\widetilde{B}(k,\widetilde{w})\bigr\|

\displaystyle=\bigl\|{\rm D}_{K}B(k,\sigma(k))+{\rm D}_{W}B(k,\sigma(k))\circ\widetilde{w}^{\prime}-{\rm D}_{K}B(k,\sigma(k))-{\rm D}_{W}B(k,\sigma(k))\circ\widetilde{w}\bigr\|

\displaystyle=\bigl\|{\rm D}_{W}B(k,\sigma(k))\circ(\widetilde{w}^{\prime}-\widetilde{w})\bigr\|\;\leq\;\theta\|\widetilde{w}^{\prime}-\widetilde{w}\|.

Thus
\widetilde{B}
satisfies (
43
) with the same
\theta
as
B
, and on the entire Banach space
\widetilde{W}=\mathcal{L}(K,\overline{W})
. And the above proof shows that
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity of
\widetilde{B}
– which would follow from
\mathcal{C}^{2}
regularity of
B
– implies
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity of
\widetilde{\sigma}={\rm D}\sigma
, and hence
\mathcal{C}^{2}
regularity of
\sigma
. We can iterate this argument to prove the Lemma for all
\ell
.

Indeed, assume by induction that
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity of a contraction for any
\ell\leq L
implies
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
regularity of the solution map. Then consider a contraction
B
of regularity
\mathcal{C}^{L+1}
. As above, the differential of its solution map
{\rm D}\sigma=\widetilde{\sigma}
is the solution map of a contraction of the form
\widetilde{B}:\mathcal{V}_{K}\times\widetilde{W}\to\widetilde{W}
, which is of regularity
\mathcal{C}^{L}
. Then the induction hypothesis ensures that its solution map
\widetilde{\sigma}={\rm D}\sigma
is of regularity
\mathcal{C}^{L}
, and hence
\sigma
is of regularity
\mathcal{C}^{L+1}
. This finishes the induction and thus the proof.
∎

Finally, we are prepared for the technical core of this paper, where we specify the meaning of adiabatic regularity for the solution maps – and show how it follows from the adiabatic regularity of the adiabatic Fredholm family as formalized in Definition
2.13
.
Note here that the crucial contribution of this paper is not just in proving this implication, but in doing so for a notion of adiabatic regularity that (a) is satisfied in Examples
1.1
and (b) yields a reasonably regular finite dimensional reduction in §
4.4
. The latter in particular requires continuity of the global solution map
(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\mapsto\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k})
– and its derivatives in
\mathfrak{k}
– in some global topology. This is what the following theorem establishes with the property of [Continuity w.r.t.
\|\cdot\|_{0}
].
To readers interested in strengthening the results or weakening the assumptions we recommend starting with this proof in (
52
) and (
57
) and reading backwards to analyze its ingredients.

Theorem 4.10
.

Given an adiabatic Fredholm family
\bigl((\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\to\Omega)_{\epsilon\in\Delta},\ldots\bigr)
as in Definition
2.1
, the contractions
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}:\mathfrak{K}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\to\overline{W}_{\epsilon}
from Lemma
4.8
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
4.9
, resulting in solution maps
\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\bigr)_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{\sigma}}

defined on neighbourhoods
\Delta_{\sigma}\subset\Delta
of
0
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\subset\mathfrak{K}
of
0

such that for some
0<\delta_{\sigma}\leq\delta_{Q}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\{(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{c},\gamma)\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\times\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{\Gamma},\epsilon}\,\big|\,\overline{\pi}_{\mathfrak{K}}(\gamma)=\mathfrak{k},\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=\mathfrak{c},\|\mathfrak{c}\|^{\mathfrak{C}}+\|\gamma\|^{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}<\delta_{\sigma}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\;\bigl\{(\mathfrak{k},w)\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\,\big|\,w-\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k},w)=0,\|w\|^{W}_{\epsilon}<\delta_{\sigma}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\;\bigl\{(\mathfrak{k},w)\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}\,\big|\,\overline{g}_{\epsilon,\mathfrak{k}}(w)=0,\|w\|^{W}_{\epsilon}<\delta_{\sigma}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\mathfrak{k},\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}))\,\big|\,\mathfrak{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\bigr\}.

Moreover, each solution map
\sigma_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}
is continuous and
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, and the family of solution maps
(\sigma_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in\Delta_{\sigma}}
is uniformly bounded and uniformly continuous:

\bigl\|\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}_{0})\bigr\|^{W}_{\epsilon}<\delta_{\sigma}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\bigl\|\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k})-\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k}_{0})\bigr\|^{W}_{\epsilon}\leq\tfrac{C_{Q}}{1-\theta}\|\mathfrak{k}-\mathfrak{k}_{0}\|^{\mathfrak{K}}\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\epsilon\in\Delta_{\sigma},\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k}_{0}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}

with the contraction constant
\theta<1
from Lemma
4.8
and the uniform constant
C_{Q}
from (
34
) .

If the adiabatic Fredholm family is fibrewise
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular as in Definition
2.10
for some
\ell>1
, then each solution map
\sigma_{\epsilon}
is
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
.

If the adiabatic Fredholm family is regularizing as in Definition
2.8
, then each solution map takes values
\sigma_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathcal{V}_{W}
in the
\epsilon
-independent dense subset
\mathcal{V}_{W}=\mathfrak{C}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}
.

If the adiabatic Fredholm family is adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular as in Definition
2.13
, then the family of solution maps
\Delta_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\mathcal{V}_{W},(\epsilon,\mathfrak{k})\mapsto\sigma_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{k})
is adiabatic
\mathcal{C}^{\ell}
-regular in the following sense:
17
17

17

Note that the higher regularizing property is necessary to even make sense of the norm in the pointwise continuity.

[Higher Regularizing Property]

The
\ell
-fold fibrewise tangent map
\Delta_{\sigma}\times{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{W}
,
(\epsilon,\underline{\mathfrak{k}})\mapsto{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\underline{\mathfrak{k}})
takes values in the
\epsilon
-independent dense subspace
{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{W}\subset{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\overline{W},\epsilon}
.

[Pointwise Continuity in
\mathbf{\Delta}
]

\forall\epsilon_{0}\in\Delta_{\sigma},\underline{\mathfrak{k}}_{0}\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl\|{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\underline{\mathfrak{k}}_{0})-{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon_{0}}(\underline{\mathfrak{k}}_{0})\bigr\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}W}_{\epsilon}\underset{\epsilon\to\epsilon_{0}}{\longrightarrow}0.

[Uniform Continuity]

There are monotone continuous functions
c^{\ell}_{\sigma}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

and
b^{\ell}_{\sigma}:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
with
c^{\ell}_{\sigma}(0)=0

so that for all
\epsilon\in\Delta_{\sigma}
and
\underline{\mathfrak{k}},\underline{\mathfrak{l}}\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}
we have
18
18

18

Here the
b^{\ell}_{\sigma}
factor accounts for the fact that tangent maps have mixed scaling properties with respect to the unbounded vector entries, as discussed in Remark
2.6
.

\bigl\|{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\underline{\mathfrak{l}})-{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\underline{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}W}_{\epsilon}\leq c^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\|\underline{\mathfrak{l}}-\underline{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathfrak{K}})\,b^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\max\{\|\mathfrak{l}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}\mathfrak{K}},\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}\mathfrak{K}}\}).

In particular, this guarantees

[Continuity w.r.t.
\mathbf{\|\cdot\|_{0}}
]

(\epsilon,\underline{\mathfrak{k}})\mapsto{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\underline{\mathfrak{k}})
is a continuous map

\Delta_{\sigma}\times{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}\to\bigl({\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{W},\|\cdot\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}W}_{0}\bigr)
.

[Uniform Bound]

For all
\epsilon\in\Delta_{\sigma}
and
\underline{\mathfrak{k}}\in{\rm T}^{\ell}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{K}}
we have

(47)

\bigl\|{\rm T}^{\ell}\sigma_{\epsilon}(\underline{\mathfrak{k}})\bigr\|^{{\rm T}^{\ell}W}_{\epsilon}\leq\delta_{\sigma}+c^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\|\underline{\mathfrak{k}}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}\mathfrak{K}})\,b^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\|\mathfrak{k}\|^{{\rm T}_{\bullet}^{\ell}\mathfrak{K}}).
